```
1	/-
2	  hex0 on the control-flow IL (`LowIR.Ctrl`). The point: error handling becomes a
3	  FLAT guard cascade with `ret`, instead of the original deeply-nested ifes + the
4	  `in_idx := in_len` poison-the-loop-guard hack.
5	
6	  Compare `LowIR/Hex0Prog.lean`'s `body` (nested, every error leaf must be the last
7	  statement on its path) with `hexPath` below (a flat sequence of `ife guard (err) skip`
8	  then the work). `err code = seqs [set status; ret]` — `ret` exits the whole function.
9	
10	  Validated at the IL level (`Ctrl.exec` directly) against `Hex0.coreSpec`.
11	-/
12	import LowIR.Ctrl
13	import Hex0.Spec
14	
15	namespace LowIR.Ctrl.Hex0
16	
17	open LowIR.Ctrl
18	open Rv64i (Word Byte)
19	
20	-- registers: in_ptr=10 in_len=11 out_ptr=12 out_cap=13 ; in_idx=5 out_idx=6 chr=7
21	--            hi=28 lo=29 addr=30 tmp=31 b=8 g=15 status=14
22	-- consts: '0'=20 '9'=21 'A'=22 'F'=23 nl=24 sp=25 us=26 '#'=27 ';'=18 bad=19 off=17 one=16
23	
24	def lit (r v : Nat) : Stmt := .addi r 0 (BitVec.ofNat 12 v)
25	
26	/-- early return with a status code — replaces the old `in_idx := in_len` hack. -/
27	def err (code : Nat) : Stmt := .seq (lit 14 code) .ret
28	
29	def pnib (dst src : Reg) : Stmt :=
30	  .ife .geu src 20
31	    (.ife .geu 21 src (.sub dst src 20)
32	      (.ife .geu src 22 (.ife .geu 23 src (.sub dst src 17) (lit dst 255)) (lit dst 255)))
33	    (lit dst 255)
34	
35	def readAdv (dst : Reg) : Stmt := seqs [.add 30 10 5, .lbu dst 30 0, .addi 5 5 1]
36	
37	def cgGuard : Stmt :=
38	  .ife .lt 5 11
39	    (seqs [.add 30 10 5, .lbu 8 30 0, .ife .eq 8 24 (lit 15 0) (lit 15 1)])
40	    (lit 15 0)
41	
42	def skipComment : Stmt :=
43	  .seq cgGuard (.while .geu 15 16 (.seq (.addi 5 5 1) cgGuard))
44	
45	/-- The hex-digit path — now a FLAT cascade: each guard `ret`s on failure (else `skip`
46	    and fall through), then the byte is written. No nesting, no poisoned guard. -/
47	def hexPath : Stmt :=
48	  seqs
49	    [ pnib 28 7,
50	      .ife .eq 28 19 (err 5) .skip,          -- bad high  → Unknown
51	      .ife .geu 5 11 (err 4) .skip,           -- no low    → Trailing
52	      readAdv 7,                             -- chr := low
53	      .ife .eq 7 24 (err 3) .skip,           -- low-stop  → Split
54	      .ife .eq 7 25 (err 3) .skip,
55	      .ife .eq 7 26 (err 3) .skip,
56	      .ife .eq 7 27 (err 3) .skip,
57	      .ife .eq 7 18 (err 3) .skip,
58	      pnib 29 7,
59	      .ife .eq 29 19 (err 5) .skip,          -- bad low   → Unknown
60	      .ife .geu 6 13 (err 2) .skip,           -- out full  → OutputShort
61	      .slli 31 28 4, .orr 31 31 29, .add 30 12 6, .sb 30 31 0, .addi 6 6 1 ]
62	
63	/-- one main-loop iteration: read a char, dispatch; falling off the end = "continue". -/
64	def body : Stmt :=
65	  .seq (readAdv 7) <|
66	  .ife .eq 7 27 skipComment <|               -- '#'  comment
67	  .ife .eq 7 18 skipComment <|               -- ';'  comment
68	  .ife .eq 7 24 .skip <|                      -- '\n' skip
69	  .ife .eq 7 25 .skip <|                      -- ' '  skip
70	  .ife .eq 7 26 .skip <|                       -- '_'  skip
71	  hexPath
72	
73	def hex0 : Stmt :=
74	  seqs
75	    [ lit 20 48, lit 21 57, lit 22 65, lit 23 70, lit 24 10, lit 25 32,
76	      lit 26 95, lit 27 35, lit 18 59, lit 19 255, lit 17 55, lit 16 1,
77	      lit 5 0, lit 6 0, lit 14 0,
78	      .while .lt 5 11 body ]
79	
80	/-! ### IL-level harness + certification against `Hex0.coreSpec`. -/
81	
82	def inBase  : Word := 0x1000
83	def outBase : Word := 0x4000
84	
85	def hex0ILState (inp : List Byte) (cap : Nat) : St :=
86	  { regs := fun i =>
87	      if i = 10 then inBase else if i = 11 then BitVec.ofNat 64 inp.length
88	      else if i = 12 then outBase else if i = 13 then BitVec.ofNat 64 cap else 0
89	    mem := fun a => let ia := (a - inBase).toNat; if ia < inp.length then (inp[ia]?).getD 0 else 0 }
90	
91	def outBytes (s : St) : List Nat :=
92	  (List.range (s.rget 6).toNat).map (fun i => (s.mem (outBase + BitVec.ofNat 64 i)).toNat)
93	
94	de
```

```
1	/-
2	  hex0 functional specification (the refinement target).
3	
4	  This MIRRORS coq/Spec.v definition-for-definition. Bytes are modelled as
5	  `Nat` (assumed < 256); the input is a `List Nat`.
6	
7	  The spec is a two-state machine (EXPECT_HIGH / EXPECT_LOW), written as a
8	  single well-founded recursion `decodeS` over an explicit state `St`, so there
9	  is exactly one termination argument (decreasing on input length).
10	
11	  In LOW position the five "stop" characters (\n ' ' '_' '#' ';') yield `Split`;
12	  any other non-hex character yields `Unknown`; EOF yields `Trailing`. Capacity
13	  (OutputShort) is NOT part of `decode` -- it is applied separately in `coreSpec`.
14	-/
15	
16	namespace Hex0
17	
18	/-- Character codes used by hex0. -/
19	def c_nl   : Nat := 10   -- '\n'
20	def c_sp   : Nat := 32   -- ' '
21	def c_us   : Nat := 95   -- '_'
22	def c_hash : Nat := 35   -- '#'
23	def c_semi : Nat := 59   -- ';'
24	
25	def isSpace   (c : Nat) : Bool := c == c_nl || c == c_sp || c == c_us
26	def isComment (c : Nat) : Bool := c == c_hash || c == c_semi
27	/-- characters that, where a low nibble is expected, give `Split`. -/
28	def isLowStop (c : Nat) : Bool := isSpace c || isComment c
29	
30	/-- nibble value 0..15, or `none` if `c` is not an uppercase hex digit. -/
31	def nibble (c : Nat) : Option Nat :=
32	  if 48 ≤ c ∧ c ≤ 57 then some (c - 48)        -- '0'..'9'
33	  else if 65 ≤ c ∧ c ≤ 70 then some (c - 55)   -- 'A'..'F' -> 10..15
34	  else none
35	
36	/-- Terminal status of a decode (capacity-independent). -/
37	inductive Status where
38	  | Ok | Split | Trailing | Unknown
39	deriving DecidableEq, Repr
40	
41	/-- Drop characters up to and including the first '\n'. -/
42	def skipComment : List Nat → List Nat
43	  | [] => []
44	  | c :: rest => if c == c_nl then rest else skipComment rest
45	
46	theorem skipComment_len : ∀ l, (skipComment l).length ≤ l.length
47	  | [] => Nat.le_refl 0
48	  | c :: rest => by
49	      simp only [skipComment]
50	      split
51	      · exact Nat.le_succ_of_le (Nat.le_refl _)
52	      · exact Nat.le_succ_of_le (skipComment_len rest)
53	
54	/-- Decoder state. -/
55	inductive St where
56	  | High | Low (hi : Nat)
57	
58	def decodeS : St → List Nat → (List Nat × Status)
59	  | .High, [] => ([], .Ok)
60	  | .Low _, [] => ([], .Trailing)
61	  | .High, c :: rest =>
62	      if isComment c then decodeS .High (skipComment rest)
63	      else if isSpace c then decodeS .High rest
64	      else match nibble c with
65	           | none => ([], .Unknown)
66	           | some hi => decodeS (.Low hi) rest
67	  | .Low hi, c :: rest =>
68	      if isLowStop c then ([], .Split)
69	      else match nibble c with
70	           | none => ([], .Unknown)
71	           | some lo =>
72	               let (out, st) := decodeS .High rest
73	               ((hi * 16 + lo) :: out, st)
74	  termination_by _ l => l.length
75	  decreasing_by
76	    all_goals simp_wf
77	    · have h := skipComment_len rest; omega
78	    all_goals omega
79	
80	def decode (l : List Nat) : List Nat × Status := decodeS .High l
81	
82	/-- Numeric status codes, matching the Error enum in hex0.c / core.s. -/
83	def statusCode : Status → Nat
84	  | .Ok => 0 | .Split => 3 | .Trailing => 4 | .Unknown => 5
85	
86	/-- Behaviour of the bounded machine `core` with output capacity `cap`:
87	    if the decode would emit more than `cap` bytes, the machine stops with
88	    OutputShort (code 2) having written exactly the first `cap` bytes.
89	    Returns (status, output, out_len). -/
90	def coreSpec (input : List Nat) (cap : Nat) : Nat × List Nat × Nat :=
91	  let (bs, st) := decode input
92	  if cap < bs.length then (2, bs.take cap, cap)
93	  else (statusCode st, bs, bs.length)
94	
95	end Hex0
96	
```

```
1	/-
2	  LowIR.Ctrl — LowIR extended with structured non-local control flow, as designed:
3	
4	    block  body          -- break scope
5	    while  c a b body     -- continue scope (the loop)
6	    brkB k               -- break: exit the k-th enclosing BLOCK (loops transparent)
7	    contL k              -- continue: restart the k-th enclosing LOOP (blocks transparent)
8	    ret                  -- return from the function (absolute; caught only at the boundary)
9	
10	  Semantics thread an `Outcome` through a clocked big-step `exec`. `ret` is the
11	  maximally-non-local outcome: every block/loop/seq/ife passes it through unchanged,
12	  caught only by the function-level `run`. `brkB`/`contL` use two independent de Bruijn
13	  index spaces (CompCert-style; LowIR is a compile target, so positional indices are fine).
14	
15	  Built additively over `LowIR` (reuses `St`/`Cond`/`evalCond`); the original
16	  `LowIR` IL + compiler + proofs are left intact for comparison.
17	-/
18	import LowIR
19	
20	namespace LowIR.Ctrl
21	
22	open LowIR (St Cond evalCond)
23	open Rv64i (Word Byte)
24	
25	abbrev Reg := Nat
26	
27	inductive Stmt where
28	  | skip
29	  | seq    (a b : Stmt)
30	  | addi   (rd rs : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)
31	  | add    (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)
32	  | sub    (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)
33	  | orr    (rd rs1 rs2 : Reg)
34	  | slli   (rd rs : Reg) (sh : Nat)
35	  | srli   (rd rs : Reg) (sh : Nat)
36	  | lbu    (rd rs : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)
37	  | sb     (rbase rval : Reg) (imm : BitVec 12)
38	  | ife    (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (t e : Stmt)
39	  | while  (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (body : Stmt)   -- continue scope
40	  | block  (body : Stmt)                           -- break scope
41	  | brkB   (k : Nat)                               -- exit k-th enclosing block
42	  | contL  (k : Nat)                               -- restart k-th enclosing loop
43	  | ret                                            -- return from function
44	deriving Repr
45	
46	/-- Control outcome of a statement. -/
47	inductive Outcome where
48	  | normal | brk (k : Nat) | cont (k : Nat) | ret
49	deriving DecidableEq, Repr
50	
51	/-- n-ary block as a smart constructor (keeps the core binary). -/
52	def block_ : List Stmt → Stmt := fun xs => .block (List.foldr .seq .skip xs)
53	def seqs   : List Stmt → Stmt := List.foldr .seq .skip
54	
55	/-- Clocked big-step semantics with an outcome. Every recursive call is at `fuel`
56	    from `fuel+1` (structurally terminating, executable). -/
57	def exec : Nat → Stmt → St → Option (St × Outcome)
58	  | 0,      _,    _ => none
59	  | fuel+1, stmt, s =>
60	    match stmt with
61	    | .skip            => some (s, .normal)
62	    | .addi rd rs imm  => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64), .normal)
63	    | .add  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 + s.rget r2), .normal)
64	    | .sub  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 - s.rget r2), .normal)
65	    | .orr  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 ||| s.rget r2), .normal)
66	    | .slli rd rs sh   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs <<< sh), .normal)
67	    | .srli rd rs sh   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs >>> sh), .normal)
68	    | .lbu  rd rs imm  => let a := s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64
69	                          some (s.rset rd ((s.loadByte a).setWidth 64), .normal)
70	    | .sb   rb rv imm  => let a := s.rget rb + imm.signExtend 64
71	                          some (s.storeByte a ((s.rget rv).setWidth 8), .normal)
72	    | .ife c a b t e   =>
73	        if evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b) then exec fuel t s else exec fuel e s
74	    | .seq a b         =>
75	        match exec fuel a s with
76	        | some (s', .normal) => exec fuel b s'
77	        | other              => other                       -- brk/cont/ret skip b
78	    | .block body      =>
79	        match exec fuel body s with
80	        | some (s', .normal)     => some (s', .normal)
81	        | some (s', .brk 0)      => some (s', .normal)       -- caught here
82	        | some (s', .brk (k+1))  => som
```

```
7	  carrying a `.normal` everywhere. The control-flow IL pays off for hex0/strtoull
8	  (early returns), not for a single straight loop like strlen.
9	
10	  Reuses the register/arith/condition lemmas from `LowIR.StrlenProof`.
11	-/
12	import LowIR.Ctrl
13	import LowIR.StrlenProof
14	
15	set_option linter.unusedSimpArgs false
16	
17	namespace LowIR.Ctrl
18	
19	open Rv64i (Word Byte)
20	
21	/-! ### exec equations for the new (outcome-threaded) semantics. -/
22	
23	theorem exec_addi (f rd rs : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12) (s : St) :
24	    exec (f+1) (.addi rd rs imm) s = some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64), .normal) := by
25	  simp [exec]
26	
27	theorem exec_sub (f rd r1 r2 : Nat) (s : St) :
28	    exec (f+1) (.sub rd r1 r2) s = some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 - s.rget r2), .normal) := by simp [exec]
29	
30	theorem exec_lbu (f rd rs : Nat) (imm : BitVec 12) (s : St) :
31	    exec (f+1) (.lbu rd rs imm) s
32	      = some (s.rset rd ((s.loadByte (s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64)).setWidth 64), .normal) := by
33	  simp [exec]
34	
35	/-- Sequence where the first part finishes `normal`. -/
36	theorem exec_seq_normal (f : Nat) (a b : Stmt) (s s' : St)
37	    (h : exec f a s = some (s', .normal)) :
38	    exec (f+1) (.seq a b) s = exec f b s' := by simp [exec, h]
39	
40	theorem exec_while_step (f : Nat) (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (body : Stmt) (s s' : St)
41	    (hc : evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b) = true) (hb : exec f body s = some (s', .normal)) :
42	    exec (f+1) (.while c a b body) s = exec f (.while c a b body) s' := by
43	  simp [exec, hc, hb]
44	
45	theorem exec_while_done (f : Nat) (c : Cond) (a b : Reg) (body : Stmt) (s : St)
46	    (hc : evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b) = false) :
47	    exec (f+1) (.while c a b body) s = some (s, .normal) := by simp [exec, hc]
48	
49	/-! ### strlen on the new IL — same program, outcome-carrying proof. -/
50	
51	def lbody : Stmt := .seq (.addi 5 5 1) (.lbu 6 5 0)
52	
53	def strlen : Stmt :=
54	  .seq (.addi 7 0 1) <| .seq (.addi 5 10 0) <| .seq (.lbu 6 5 0) <|
55	  .seq (.while .geu 6 7 lbody) (.sub 12 5 10)
56	
57	theorem strlen_loop (n : Nat) :
58	    ∀ (st : St) (cur : Word),
59	      st.rget 5 = cur → st.rget 7 = 1 → st.rget 6 = (st.mem cur).setWidth 64 →
60	      (∀ k, k < n → st.mem (cur + BitVec.ofNat 64 k) ≠ 0) →
61	      st.mem (cur + BitVec.ofNat 64 n) = 0 →
62	      ∃ st', exec (n + 2) (.while .geu 6 7 lbody) st = some (st', .normal)
63	        ∧ st'.rget 5 = cur + BitVec.ofNat 64 n
64	        ∧ st'.rget 10 = st.rget 10
65	        ∧ st'.mem = st.mem := by
66	  induction n with
67	  | zero =>
68	    intro st cur h5 h7 h6 _ hz
69	    rw [cur_zero] at hz

72	           by rw [cur_zero]; exact h5, rfl, rfl⟩

74	    intro st cur h5 h7 h6 hpre hz
75	    have hb0 : st.mem cur ≠ 0 := by have := hpre 0 (by omega); rwa [cur_zero] at this

78	      rw [exec_addi]; simp [h5, one_signExtend]
79	    have hstep : exec (n+2) lbody st
80	        = some ((st.rset 5 (cur+1)).rset 6 ((st.mem (cur+1)).setWidth 64), .normal) := by
81	      show exec (n+2) (.seq (.addi 5 5 1) (.lbu 6 5 0)) st = _
82	      rw [show n+2 = (n+1)+1 from rfl, exec_seq_normal _ _ _ _ _ ha, exec_lbu]
83	      simp [zero_signExtend, wadd_zero]
```

```
4	
5	  The headline: the reasoning primitives for `break`/`block`/`ret` are *trivial*. Each

7	  `normal`, a `while` passing `brk` through, a `seq` short-circuiting on `brk`. So the
8	  outcome machinery costs almost nothing to reason about — it just adds a few uniform
9	  equations alongside the `normal` ones we already used for strlen.
10	
11	  The functional proof (`strtoull10_correct`) is then a `digit_loop` invariant +
12	  induction on the number of leading digits — the same shape as `strlen`'s loop lemma,
13	  with two additions: the accumulator step `acc*10 = (acc<<3)+(acc<<1)` (a BitVec
14	  identity), and the terminating iteration returning `brk 0` (caught by the `block`).
15	  Stated with the invariant here; the induction is deferred (`sorry`), backed by the
16	  executable certification `strtoull10_matches_spec`.

26	
27	theorem exec_slli (f rd rs sh : Nat) (s : St) :
28	    exec (f+1) (.slli rd rs sh) s = some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs <<< sh), .normal) := by simp [exec]
29	
30	theorem exec_add (f rd r1 r2 : Nat) (s : St) :
31	    exec (f+1) (.add rd r1 r2) s = some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 + s.rget r2), .normal) := by simp [exec]
32	
33	theorem exec_orr (f rd r1 r2 : Nat) (s : St) :
34	    exec (f+1) (.orr rd r1 r2) s = some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 ||| s.rget r2), .normal) := by simp [exec]
35	
36	theorem exec_brkB (f k : Nat) (s : St) :
37	    exec (f+1) (.brkB k) s = some (s, .brk k) := by simp [exec]
38	
39	theorem exec_ret (f : Nat) (s : St) : exec (f+1) .ret s = some (s, .ret) := by simp [exec]
40	
41	/-- `seq` short-circuits on a break (the heart of the flat error cascade). -/
42	theorem exec_seq_brk (f : Nat) (a b : Stmt) (s s' : St) (k : Nat)
43	    (h : exec f a s = some (s', .brk k)) :

45
```

```
68	structure St where
69	  regs : Reg → Word
70	  mem  : Word → Byte
71	
72	@[inline] def St.rget (s : St) (i : Reg) : Word := if i = 0 then 0 else s.regs i
73	
74	@[inline] def St.rset (s : St) (i : Reg) (v : Word) : St :=
75	  if i = 0 then s else { s with regs := fun j => if j = i then v else s.regs j }
76	
77	@[inline] def St.loadByte (s : St) (a : Word) : Byte := s.mem a
78	
79	@[inline] def St.storeByte (s : St) (a : Word) (b : Byte) : St :=
80	  { s with mem := fun x => if x = a then b else s.mem x }
81	
82	/-- Evaluate a relational condition between two register values. -/
83	@[inline] def evalCond (c : Cond) (x y : Word) : Bool :=
84	  match c with
85	  | .eq  => x = y
86	  | .lt  => x.slt y
87	  | .ge  => !x.slt y
88	  | .geu => !x.ult y
89	
90	/-- Clocked big-step semantics. `fuel` bounds nesting depth of evaluation; `none`
91	    means the fuel ran out (never reached on a well-fuelled terminating program).
92	    Every recursive call is at `fuel` from `fuel+1`, so this is structurally
93	    terminating AND directly executable. Proofs induct on `fuel` / on `Stmt`. -/
94	def exec : Nat → Stmt → St → Option St
95	  | 0,      _,    _ => none
96	  | fuel+1, stmt, s =>
97	    match stmt with
98	    | .skip            => some s
99	    | .seq a b         => (exec fuel a s).bind (exec fuel b)
100	    | .addi rd rs imm  => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64))
101	    | .add  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 + s.rget r2))
102	    | .sub  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 - s.rget r2))
103	    | .orr  rd r1 r2   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget r1 ||| s.rget r2))
104	    | .slli rd rs sh   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs <<< sh))
105	    | .srli rd rs sh   => some (s.rset rd (s.rget rs >>> sh))
106	    | .lbu  rd rs imm  =>
107	        let a := s.rget rs + imm.signExtend 64
108	        some (s.rset rd ((s.loadByte a).setWidth 64))
109	    | .sb   rb rv imm  =>
110	        let a := s.rget rb + imm.signExtend 64
111	        some (s.storeByte a ((s.rget rv).setWidth 8))
112	    | .ife c a b t e   =>
113	        if evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b) then exec fuel t s else exec fuel e s
114	    | .while c a b body =>
115	        if evalCond c (s.rget a) (s.rget b)
116	        then (exec fuel body s).bind (exec fuel (.while c a b body))
117	        else some s
118	
119	/-- Run with a large default fuel (executable validation convenience). -/
120	def run (p : Stmt) (s : St) : Option St := exec 100000 p s
121	
122	/-! ### Demo: `strlen` written in LowIR
123	
124	    Registers: x10 = s (argument, the string pointer); x5 = cursor; x6 = current
125	    byte; x7 = the constant 1; x12 = result length.
126	
127	    `while (x6 >=u 1)` runs exactly while the current byte is non-zero (a zero byte
128	    is `< 1` unsigned), i.e. it stops at the NUL terminator — a faithful `strlen`. -/
129	def strlen : Stmt :=
130	  .seq (.addi 7 0 1) <|            -- x7 := 1
131	  .seq (.addi 5 10 0) <|           -- x5 := s            (cursor := s)
132	  .seq (.lbu 6 5 0) <|             -- x6 := mem[x5]      (load first byte)
133	  .seq (.while .geu 6 7            -- while (x6 >=u 1):
134	        (.seq (.addi 5 5 1)        --   x5 := x5 + 1     (advance)
135	              (.lbu 6 5 0))) <|    --   x6 := mem[x5]    (reload)
136	  (.sub 12 5 10)                   -- x12 := x5 - s      (length)
137	
138	/-- A test state: the bytes of `s` laid out at address `base`, NUL-terminated. -/
139	def memOf (base : Word) (bytes : List Byte) : Word → Byte :=
140	  fun a => (bytes[(a - base).toNat]?).getD 0
141	
142	def stateWith (base : Word) (bytes : List Byte) : St :=
143	  { regs := fun i => if i = 10 then base else 0, mem := memOf base bytes }
144	
145	-- "ABC" then NUL (length 3) at address 0x1000.
146	def demoBytes : List Byte := [0x41, 0x42, 0x43, 0x00]
147	
148	#guard
149	  match run strlen (stateWith 0x1000 demoBytes) with
150	  | some s => (s.rget 12).toNat == 3
151	  | none   => false
152	
153	-- empty string "" (just NUL) → length 0
154	#guard
```

```
74	- **`cgGuard_eff`** — comment-loop guard: `x15 = gOf = (in_idx<in_len ∧ mem[p+i]≠'\n') ? 1 : 0`,
75	  preserves all regs except scratch {8,15,30}.
76	- **`skip_body`** (one comment iteration), **`skip_loop`** (the `while`, induction on `d`),
77	  **`skipComment_eff`** (full skip: advances `in_idx` by `d` to the first `\n`/EOF).
78	
79	### Remaining (the semantic core — the hard part)
80	
81	**(A) body-dispatch step.** `CtrlHex0.body = readAdv 7; <dispatch chain> ; hexPath`. Prove a
82	`body_step` lemma: one main-loop iteration corresponds to **one `decodeS` token**. Structure of
83	the dispatch (after reading `chr` and `in_idx++`):
84	- `chr ∈ {#(35), ;(59)}` → `skipComment` (use `skipComment_eff`), body returns `.normal` (loop continues).
85	- `chr ∈ {\n(10), space(32), _(95)}` → `.skip`, `.normal` (loop continues).
86	- else (hex digit path `hexPath`): `pnib 28 7` (hi, use `pnib_correct`); if `hi=255` → `ret 5`
87	  (Unknown); if `in_idx≥in_len` → `ret 4` (Trailing); `readAdv 7` (lo); if `chr` is a low-stop
88	  `{\n,sp,_,#,;}` → `ret 3` (Split); `pnib 29 7` (lo); if `lo=255` → `ret 5`; if `out_idx≥out_cap`
89	  → `ret 2` (OutputShort); else write `(hi<<4)|lo` to `out[out_idx]`, `out_idx++`. (`hexPath` is a
90	  **flat `ife guard (err) skip` cascade** — see `CtrlHex0.lean`; `err code = lit 14 code; ret`.)
91	  
92	  Note the char checks use `.eq` (= `decide (x = y)` — easy) and the digit path emits a byte via
93	  `slli 31 28 4; orr 31 31 29; sb` (`hi*16+lo`).
94	
95	**(B) main-loop invariant** (`while .lt 5 11 body`). At each loop head the IL is at a *High*
96	position, so `decodeS High input` splits there:
97	- `in_idx = i`, `out_idx = |produced|`, `x14 = 0` (Ok so far);
98	- output region `[out_ptr, out_ptr+out_idx)` = the bytes decoded from `input[0..i)`;
99	- `decodeS High input = (produced ++ rest, finalStatus)` where `(rest, finalStatus) = decodeS High (input.drop i)`;
100	- `i ≤ in_len`, `out_idx ≤ cap`, input memory unchanged.
101	Induct (like `digit_loop`) on `in_len - i` (or on `(input.drop i).length`). The main loop uses
102	`.lt 5 11` (signed) → use `slt_true/slt_false` with `i ≤ in_len < 2^63` carried in the invariant.
103	**Comment subtlety:** IL `skipComment` stops *at* the `\n` (doesn't consume it); the spec
104	`Hex0.skipComment` consumes *through* the `\n`. They reconcile because the *next* main-loop
105	iteration reads that `\n` as a space and skips it — so the per-token correspondence for a comment
106	spans `skipComment_eff` + one more space iteration. Handle this in the invariant/induction.
107	
108	**(C) coreSpec assembly.** `coreSpec` computes the *full* decode bytes then checks `cap < bytes.length`;
109	the IL checks capacity per byte and `ret`s `OutputShort` on the first overflow. These agree because
110	the IL hits whichever of {capacity, decode-error} comes first, left-to-right, and "capacity on full
111	length applied after" is equivalent (if `bytes.length > cap`, capacity is hit before any later error;
112	else the decode status stands). Prove this equivalence, then peel hex0's 15-instruction const/init
113	prelude into the loop-entry state and apply the invariant; relate `x14`/output/`x6` to `coreSpec`.
114	
115	The IL harness for the spec relation is `CtrlHex0.hex0ILState`/`hex0Run` (input at `inBase=0x1000`,
116	output at `outBase=0x4000`, regs 10–13). Memory past `inp.length` reads 0.
117	
```

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  06:43:26; model 35.8s

> 💭 (thinking)

Committed. Now your errno point — and the good news first: **it doesn't touch the hex0 work at all.** hex0 never sets errno; this only bears on the *conformant* `strtoull` functional proof, which is still the deferred item in `CtrlStrtoull2Proof.lean`. So nothing already proved is affected.

On the substance, you're right that errno is the one place the "flat `mem : Word → Byte`" model meets a libc wart. Let me lay out what's actually true and the cleanest way through:

**Why it feels scary:** In modern glibc/musl, `errno` is a macro for `*__errno_location()`, and `__errno_location()` hands back a pointer into thread-local storage (`__thread int`). So *in a multithreaded libc* errno genuinely is per-thread, and modeling it faithfully would drag in TLS/`%fs`-relative addressing — which your LowIR memory model has no notion of.

**Why it's actually fine here:** this is a hex0 bootstrap. There are no threads at this stage — no `pthread_create`, no `clone(CLONE_VM|...)`, no TLS setup. In a single-threaded program, errno *is* legitimately just a global `int` at a fixed address, and that's exactly how minimal/bootstrap libcs implement it (a plain `.bss` global, `__errno_location` returns `&errno`). So the faithful model is: errno = a store to one fixed global address. No TLS machinery, no thread reasoning in the proof — just an `sb`/`sw` to a constant address.

**So the choice is a documented assumption, not a proof obligation.** Two ways to discharge it, in order of preference:

1. **Assume single-threaded, errno = fixed global (recommended).** Add it to `docs/TCB.md` as an explicit assumption: "the bootstrap is single-threaded; `errno` is a global `int`, not TLS." Then `CtrlStrtoull2`'s overflow path is just `lit <errno_addr>; sw ...ERANGE...`, and the spec's `coreSpec`-analogue returns `(ULLONG_MAX, errno := ERANGE)` as an extra memory-write component. The functional proof tracks one more `storeByte`/`storeWord` — no harder than hex0's output-byte write.

2. **Abstract the errno cell.** Have the spec say "errno-location holds ERANGE afterward" without committing to where it lives — the refinement claims the *value* written to whatever address `__errno_location` yields. This defers the single-threaded commitment but needs you to model `__errno_location` as returning a stable pointer, which is more machinery for no real gain in a bootstrap.

I'd go with (1): it's the truthful model for this target, it's one line in the TCB, and it keeps the memory model flat. If you ever add threads, errno graduates to TLS and *that's* when you'd revisit it — but that's a different libc than the one you're bootstrapping.

Want me to write that assumption into `docs/TCB.md` now so it's recorded before you pick the conformant-strtoull work back up? It's independent of the hex0 proof, which I can keep pushing on (the main-loop invariant is next).

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  06:47:58; model 1m25s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Right — and this is the deeper, more important realization. Globals don't just need a *location*, they need **separation**: the moment a function mutates a global *and* touches caller-provided pointers, the spec is only true under non-aliasing preconditions. A flat `mem : Word → Byte` is one address space, so "write errno" and "write `*dst`" hit the same map, and if their addresses coincide the postcondition you wanted is just false.

A few things worth separating out:

**1. This isn't an errno problem — it's the globals-meet-pointers problem, and hex0 already has it.** hex0 reads the input region `[inBase, inBase+len)` and writes the output region `[outBase, outBase+cap)`. The functional spec `coreSpec` works on the pure input *list*. For the IL to match it, writing output byte `k` must not change an input byte the parser hasn't read yet — i.e. the two regions must be disjoint. So the main-loop invariant (item B, the thing I'm about to build) *already* has to carry a disjointness fact. With the concrete harness (`inBase=0x1000`, `outBase=0x4000`) that's `inBase + len ≤ outBase` (input fits below output, so output writes at ≥0x4000 never alias input at <0x1000+len when `len ≤ 0x3000`). errno, when we get to conformant strtoull, is *the same machinery with one more single-address region* — not a new category of difficulty.

**2. The `sprintf(&errno, ...)` case is a precondition violation, and the model is *right* to refuse it.** You don't prevent it. `sprintf`'s correctness theorem has a precondition that its write footprint (the `dst` buffer) is a region the caller owns and that's disjoint from anything the postcondition claims to preserve. If the caller passes `dst = __errno_location()`, that precondition is unmet, so the theorem promises nothing — exactly as C intends (it's the caller's bug). Crucially: `sprintf` itself doesn't *write* errno on success, so its footprint is just `dst`; "sprintf into &errno" is then well-defined (errno ends up holding the formatted bytes) and not even pathological. The genuine alias hazard is only a function that writes **both** errno and a caller pointer — e.g. `strtoull` writing errno *and* `*endptr` — where you need `endptr ≠ &errno` as a precondition (or you specify last-writer-wins).

**3. The scalable discipline: reserve a region for libc globals, prove disjointness once.** Per-call disjointness hypotheses get unwieldy. Instead, give libc globals (errno, and later allocator metadata, etc.) a fixed reserved region the program's memory map never hands out as user buffers — exactly what a real linker does putting `.bss` somewhere the heap/stack don't reach. Then every spec carries one standing invariant ("user regions ⊆ user-area, globals ⊆ reserved-area, the two areas are disjoint") and individual disjointness facts fall out of it, rather than being threaded call-by-call. This is the CompCert/Bedrock2/VST move (separation logic / explicit `Disjoint` region facts); you can do a lightweight version with a couple of interval-disjointness lemmas over `Word`.

So: the flat model survives; what you've discovered is that **the first global forces you to adopt a memory-region/ownership discipline** — and that's inherent to C, not a modeling mistake.

Concretely for where we are: I'll build the input/output disjointness into the item-B invariant anyway (it's unavoidable for hex0), which means the region-separation lemmas I write there are exactly the ones conformant-strtoull's errno will reuse. Want me to:
- **(a)** record this separation discipline + the single-threaded-errno assumption in `docs/TCB.md` now, and
- **(b)** keep going on item B with the disjointness hypothesis stated as `inBase + len ≤ outBase` (concrete) or a general `Disjoint` predicate (reusable for errno later)?

My recommendation is the general `Disjoint`-predicate form so it's reused, not rebuilt. Which would you like?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  06:52:54; model 1m17s

> 💭 (thinking)

Read the first four pages — that's enough to see why you reached for it, and I think you're right that it's the correct *vocabulary*, with one important scoping caveat. Let me lay out what TB buys here, then a concrete, tractable way to put it "in the spec."

**Why TB is the right lens (and not just raw `Disjoint`).** The naive fix I floated — "assume `inBase+len ≤ outBase`" — is both too weak (it's an ad-hoc fact, not a contract that composes across libc) and, in the other direction, too *strong* as a general discipline: pure disjointness forbids two functions from each holding a read view of the same `const char *`. TB gives exactly the asymmetry a libc needs:

- **shared borrow (input, `&[u8]`)**: read-only; *may* overlap other shared borrows; the bytes are stable for its lifetime. This is precisely the `const char *nptr` / `const void *` story — two readers of the same literal must be allowed.
- **unique borrow (output, `&mut [u8]`)**: exclusive; must be disjoint from *every* other live borrow (shared or unique). This is what makes "writing output never perturbs input" a *theorem about borrow well-formedness* rather than a hypothesis I smuggle in.
- the two TB features beyond Stacked Borrows that matter for libc specifically: **dynamic ranges** (a pointer derived from `&arr[0]` may walk the whole slice — the C idiom, exactly what `strtoull` does to `nptr`) and **read-read reordering** (shared views compose).

So `hex0 : (input : &[u8]) → (out : &mut [u8]) → Status` *as a borrow signature* is the spec, and `Disjoint input out` falls out of "`out` is unique."

**errno drops out cleanly.** errno is a `&mut`-borrow held by libc internals. By well-formedness it's disjoint from any caller borrow. And the `sprintf(&errno, ...)` pathology becomes precise: to pass `&errno` as `dst`, the caller would need a *unique* borrow of errno's location, which conflicts with libc's own unique borrow of it → two overlapping uniques → TB-UB → the call's precondition is unmet. That's the principled "why is this illegal," not a hand-wave.

**The scoping caveat — don't port the operational model.** Full Tree Borrows is a 24-page per-location permission state machine (reserved/active transitions, the tree of derived pointers, UB triggers) with a Rocq formalization. Embedding *that* operational semantics under LowIR would dwarf every functional proof in this repo and isn't needed: hex0 doesn't create derived pointers, doesn't reborrow, isn't multithreaded. What I want is the **separation *consequence*** of TB — the well-formedness invariant and the disjointness it implies — phrased in borrow vocabulary so it composes and so it's the same machinery errno reuses.

Concretely, a thin layer (≈30 lines, reusable across the whole libc):

```lean
structure Slice where base : Word; len : Nat
def Slice.mem (s : Slice) (a : Word) : Prop := ∃ k, k < s.len ∧ a = s.base + BitVec.ofNat 64 k
inductive Perm | shared | uniq
structure Borrow where slice : Slice; perm : Perm
def Disjoint (s t : Slice) : Prop := ∀ a, s.mem a → t.mem a → False

/-- A unique borrow is disjoint from every other borrow; shared may overlap shared. -/
def Wf (bs : List Borrow) : Prop :=
  ∀ b ∈ bs, ∀ b' ∈ bs, b ≠ b' → (b.perm = .uniq ∨ b'.perm = .uniq) → Disjoint b.slice b'.slice
```

hex0's spec then takes `(hwf : Wf [⟨⟨inBase,len⟩,.shared⟩, ⟨⟨outBase,cap⟩,.uniq⟩])`, from which `Disjoint input output` is a one-liner, and that disjointness is what the item-B invariant needs for "input bytes unchanged." TB is the *semantic justification* (cited in the doc) for why this contract is sound and composable; the Lean layer is just its separation residue.

This means the disjointness work I have to do for item B anyway becomes the first instance of the borrow layer instead of throwaway.

So, two questions before I build it:
1. **Scope** — agreed that we take TB as the *contract discipline* (the `Wf`/`Disjoint` layer above), not the full operational model? (Strong recommend yes; the operational model is a separate research-scale effort.)
2. **Sequencing** — want me to (a) write a short `docs/` design note ("memory borrows: shared input / unique output, single-threaded errno, TB as justification") and define the `Slice`/`Borrow`/`Wf` layer, *then* resume item B on top of it; or (b) keep driving item B with a bare `Disjoint inBase…outBase` hypothesis now and refactor it into the borrow layer once the invariant is proved?

I lean (a) — define the small layer first so item B's hypothesis is already the real contract. Your call.

Everything builds clean and **sorry-free**. Here's where this session landed.

## What got done

**Item A (`body_step`) — complete.** Every main-loop iteration's effect is now characterized, sorry-free:
- `readAdv_eff`, `body_space` (skip chars), `body_comment` (skip-to-newline via `skipComment_eff`), and the big one — `hexPath_eff`/`body_hex`: the full hex-digit path with all **6 mutually-exclusive outcomes** proved (bad-high/trailing/split/bad-low/output-short → `ret` with status code; hex pair → byte written, cursor/out_idx advanced).

**Key infrastructure built along the way:**
- **Fuel monotonicity** (`exec_mono`/`exec_mono_le`) — lets every lemma use existential fuel and compose without exact-fuel arithmetic. This was the unlock for everything after.
- **Memory-borrow separation layer** (`Slice`/`Perm`/`Borrow`/`Wf`/`Disjoint` + `storeByte_preserves`) — the Tree-Borrows-residue we discussed: shared-input / unique-output, with `docs/MEMORY-BORROWS.md` recording the design, the single-threaded-errno assumption, and why we take the contract, not the operational model.
- **IL↔spec bridges**: `pnibR_eq_255_iff`, `pnibR_nibble`, `hexbyte_val` (`(hi<<4)|lo = hi*16+lo` via the disjoint-or lemma), `lowStop_iff`.
- **Spec unfolders**: `decodeS_high_*`/`decodeS_low_*`, and `skipComment_run`/`skipComment_nonl` (the comment-`\n` reconciliation glue).

That's ~10 commits, all green.

## What's left (item B + C), now a composition

The main-loop invariant is the remaining crux, but it's no longer open-ended — every piece it needs is proved. `docs/RESUME-LOWIR.md` §3(B)/§5 now has the fully worked-out plan: strong induction on `len - idx`, the per-arm `body_* → decodeS_*` mapping, the comment reconciliation (the `\n` subtlety *dissolves* — the IH from the newline position discharges it directly), the input/output memory bridge, and the capacity split that feeds item C. A notable simplification I worked out: stating the loop lemma on `decodeS High (inp.drop idx)` means **no separate High-boundary decomposition lemma is needed** — it's trivial at `idx=0`.

On your errno/aliasing thread: it's fully addressed in the design — errno is just one more unique borrow, and the input/output disjointness item B needs anyway is the first instance of exactly that machinery, so it's not throwaway.

I stopped short of grinding the invariant induction itself in this turn — it's a multi-lemma effort (Word-index/memory bridge + the induction) best done fresh against this now-complete foundation rather than rushed. Want me to start it next, or would you rather I pick up the conformant-strtoull/errno work now that the borrow discipline is settled?

```
25	structure Slice where
26	  base : Word
27	  len  : Nat
28	
29	/-- Addresses covered by a slice. -/
30	def Slice.has (s : Slice) (a : Word) : Prop := ∃ k, k < s.len ∧ a = s.base + BitVec.ofNat 64 k
31	
32	inductive Perm | shared | uniq
33	deriving DecidableEq, Repr
34	
35	structure Borrow where
36	  slice : Slice
37	  perm  : Perm
38	
39	def Disjoint (s t : Slice) : Prop := ∀ a, s.has a → t.has a → False
40	
41	/-- Well-formed borrow set: a unique borrow is disjoint from every other borrow. -/
42	def Wf (bs : List Borrow) : Prop :=
43	  ∀ b ∈ bs, ∀ b' ∈ bs, b ≠ b' → (b.perm = .uniq ∨ b'.perm = .uniq) → Disjoint b.slice b'.slice
44	
45	theorem Disjoint.symm {s t : Slice} (h : Disjoint s t) : Disjoint t s :=
46	  fun a hat has => h a has hat
```

## What was proved

```lean
theorem hex0_correct (inp : List Nat) (cap : Nat)
    (hinp : ∀ x ∈ inp, x < 256) (hlen : inp.length < 2^63) (hcap : cap < 2^63)
    (hwf : Wf [⟨⟨inBase, inp.length⟩, .shared⟩, ⟨⟨outBase, cap⟩, .uniq⟩]) :
    ∃ fuel, hex0Run (asBytes inp) cap fuel = Hex0.coreSpec inp cap
```

The `hex0` program, run on the control-flow IL, computes the spec `Hex0.coreSpec` (the two-state `decodeS` recursion + capacity) on **all inputs** — replacing the `native_decide` battery with a real theorem. The only hypotheses are the natural ones (byte values < 256, sizes < 2⁶³) plus the **borrow precondition** we designed together: input is a shared borrow, output a unique borrow, so `Wf ⇒ Disjoint` and output writes provably never perturb the input.

## How it came together this session (all sorry-free)

- **Item A — `body_step`**: `body_space`, `body_comment`, and `hexPath_eff`/`body_hex` (the hex path's 6 mutually-exclusive outcomes).
- **Item B — `main_loop`**: strong induction on `len − idx` tying the `while` loop to `decodeS` via `boundedRun` (per-byte capacity). The comment-`\n` subtlety dissolved exactly as planned — the IH from the newline position discharges it.
- **Item C**: `boundedRun_nil_coreSpec` (per-byte capacity = `coreSpec`'s take-cap), `hex0_setup` (peels the 15-instruction const/init prelude), and the assembly through `run`/`outBytes`.

Plus the reusable infrastructure: fuel monotonicity (`exec_mono`), the Tree-Borrows-residue separation layer (`Slice`/`Wf`/`Disjoint`/`storeByte_preserves`, documented in `docs/MEMORY-BORROWS.md`), the IL↔spec bridges (`pnibR_nibble`, `hexbyte_val`, `lowStop_iff`), and the `decodeS` unfolders.

Your two design calls both paid off concretely: the **borrow framing** became the actual disjointness hypothesis the proof needs (not throwaway), and the **single-threaded-errno** assumption is recorded for when the conformant-strtoull work picks up.

Full build is green (`Build completed successfully`), 24 commits, the only `sorry` in the tree is the long-sanctioned `compile_sim` (T1). `docs/RESUME-LOWIR.md` now marks hex0 done and points the next session at conformant strtoull and `compile_sim`.

### 👤 **User**  ·  03:43:37; waited 14m54s

Can you tell me if introducing the IL has simplified the proof at all?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  03:44:19; model 1m04s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Honest answer: **yes, but specifically for the functional layer, and partly by deferring complexity rather than eliminating it.** Let me separate the real wins from the caveats, grounded in what's actually in the repo.

## Where the IL genuinely simplified things

**1. Structured `ret`/`while`/`block` turned error handling into flat short-circuiting.** This is the biggest, most concrete win. hex0 has five early-return error exits (Unknown/Trailing/Split/OutputShort). In `LowIR.Ctrl` each is just `err code = seq (lit 14 code) ret`, and the `seq` short-circuits on the `ret` outcome automatically. So in `hexPath_eff` the error arms are each ~8 lines: navigate to the failing `ife`, `exec_err`, done — the rest of the cascade is provably skipped by `exec_seq_ret`. The file's own header records the contrast: the original (non-control-flow) `LowIR` had to *nest every error leaf as the last statement on its path* plus an `in_idx := in_len` "poison-the-loop-guard" hack to force loop exit. The Ctrl version deletes that. And the reasoning primitives for it (`exec_seq_ret`, `exec_block_catch`, `exec_while_brk/ret`) are genuinely one-liners (`by simp [exec, h]`) — that was the headline finding in `CtrlStrtoullProof`.

**2. The clocked big-step `exec` + per-construct equation lemmas abstract away the whole machine.** I reasoned entirely by rewriting with `exec_addi`/`exec_seq_normal`/`exec_ife_then`/… over `rget`/`rset`/`mem`. There was no PC, no instruction fetch/decode, no branch-offset arithmetic, no encoding — all of which a direct bare-RISC-V proof of hex0 would force you to carry. For a control-flow-heavy program that's a large saving.

## The honest caveats

**3. The IL *moved* the machine-level complexity into `compile_sim`, which is still a `sorry`.** `hex0_correct` is a theorem about `LowIR.Ctrl.exec`, not about RISC-V bytes. The bridge — compile Ctrl → RV64I and prove simulation — is the sanctioned `sorry` in `LowIR.lean`. So the *total* verified story isn't simpler yet; the IL bought a clean functional proof at the cost of an unwritten compiler-correctness proof. And note the irony: the very feature that simplified hex0 (structured `while`/`block`/`ret`) is exactly what's *hard* to compile correctly to flat labels/jumps. Some of the complexity hex0 shed will reappear there. Until `compile_sim` is discharged, the IL's payoff is conditional.

**4. The clocked-fuel choice added bookkeeping.** Threading explicit `fuel` was a recurring nuisance — composing existential-fuel results across the variable-cost main loop only became tractable after I proved `exec_mono`/`exec_mono_le`. A relational (fuel-free) big-step would have avoided that entirely. So part of the friction this session was self-inflicted by the *clocked* flavor, not the IL idea per se (though `exec_mono` neutralized it once and for all).

## The nuance that sharpens the verdict

The repo already measured this: `CtrlStrlen`'s recorded finding is that for a single straight loop (strlen, no early returns), the outcome machinery is **pure overhead** — same proof shape as the plain IL, just carrying `.normal` everywhere. So the control-flow IL pays off *precisely* for functions with non-local exits (hex0, strtoull) and is a slight tax otherwise.

**Net:** for hex0 specifically, the IL was a clear win on the functional proof — mostly from `ret`-based flat error cascades and from abstracting the encoding/PC. But it's a win that relocates rather than removes the machine-level work (`compile_sim`), and the clocked semantics cost a monotonicity lemma to make ergonomic. If you discharge `compile_sim` once and reuse it across the whole libc, the IL pays for itself many times over; if you only ever verified hex0, a direct proof might have been comparable total effort.

### 👤 **User**  ·  03:48:15; waited 3m33s

But compile sim needs to be paid once per IL, and proofs for programs can reuse it?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  03:48:56; model 56.6s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Exactly right — that's the correct economic argument, and it's precisely why this architecture (a small verified IL between spec and machine) is sound: it's the CompCert bet. `compile_sim` is `∀ program, exec p s ≡ runRV64I (encode (compile p)) …`, so once proved it transports *any* functional theorem `exec p ≡ spec` to the bytes. hex0, strtoull, memcpy, … all reuse the same simulation. Pay the machine-level reasoning once, amortize across the whole libc. If you verify many programs (which is the libc plan), the IL pays for itself many times over.

But two sharp caveats keep this from being quite as clean as "it's already paid":

**1. The `compile_sim` that's currently a `sorry` is for the *original* `LowIR`, not `LowIR.Ctrl` — and hex0 is proved on `Ctrl`.** That matters because the two ILs have different power:
- `LowIR` (original): `skip/seq/addi/…/ife/while` — structured ifs and loops, but **no non-local control flow**. It has a compiler + `encode` + the `compile_sim` framework (the sorry).
- `LowIR.Ctrl`: adds `block/brk/contL/ret` with outcome threading. It has **no compiler at all** — `Ctrl.lean` is just the semantics; the header says the original IL+compiler are "left intact for comparison."

So `hex0_correct` currently has *no* path to bytes. You'd need a `compile`/`compile_sim` for `Ctrl` (or a `Ctrl → LowIR` lowering, then reuse the existing one). And `Ctrl`'s extra feature — `ret`/`block`/`brk` — is exactly what made hex0's error handling collapse into flat short-circuiting cascades. Compiling *that* to flat RV64I (returns/breaks → jumps with label resolution, the loop-exit that `ret` did for free) is the genuinely hard compiler pass — in CompCert terms, the CFG/control-lowering passes are the painful ones. So the complexity hex0 shed doesn't vanish; it relocates into the Ctrl compiler. The good news per your point: it relocates into a thing you **pay once**.

**2. "Reuse for free" is modulo uniform boundary conditions.** Even with a general `compile_sim`, transporting a per-program functional theorem isn't literally zero-cost each time: the program must be in the IL's well-formed/compilable fragment, fuel/termination has to bridge to the machine's step count, and the spec's register/memory layout has to line up with the compiled calling convention at the boundary. These are uniform and mechanical *once the framework exists* — but they're per-program glue, not nothing.

**Bottom line:** your reasoning holds — the IL's deferred machine work is a fixed cost, not per-program, and that's the right trade for a libc. The correction is just *which* fixed cost is outstanding: it's the **Ctrl** compiler + its `compile_sim`, which hasn't been started and is the hard control-flow-lowering pass (the `LowIR.lean` sorry only covers the weaker original IL). So the accurate statement is: hex0's functional proof is done and will transport *for free once you pay for the Ctrl→RV64I compiler exactly once* — and that one payment is where the control-flow complexity hex0 avoided will actually land.

### 👤 **User**  ·  03:50:21; waited 1m10s

Does the low IR have a notion of stack variables? Should it?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  03:51:13; model 1m11s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

**Currently: no.** The state is `St = { regs : Reg → Word, mem : Word → Byte }` with `Reg = Nat`, and the instruction set is `addi/add/sub/orr/slli/srli/lbu/sb/ife/while` (+ `block/brk/contL/ret` in Ctrl). So:

- **Locals live in an *unbounded* register file.** Because `Reg = Nat`, the IL has effectively infinite registers — hex0 uses x5–x31 freely and never has to spill anything.
- **Memory is flat bytes with explicit `lbu`/`sb`.** A stack *could* be modeled as a region of `mem` addressed off some SP register by convention, but there's no built-in support: no SP, no frame, no `push`/`pop`/`alloca`, no `call` (Ctrl's `ret` is "return to the function boundary," not a call/return-address mechanism).

So the IL has no stack the same way it has no PC and no instruction encoding — all three are abstracted away and deferred to `compile_sim`.

**Should it? Eventually yes, but probably not *in the IL* — and the absence has been correct so far.** Three distinct forces will demand stack discipline, and they land in different places:

1. **Register pressure (>31 live values).** This one is *already* an obligation, not optional. The "infinite register file" is a fiction the compiler must reconcile with RV64I's 32 GPRs. Any program with more simultaneously-live IL registers than fit must spill — and spilling *is* stack traffic that `compile_sim` has to prove equivalent. hex0 happens to fit in 32, so it dodges this, but in general the stack is *already implied at the compile boundary* even with zero IL-level stack support. **This should stay in the compiler** (register allocation + spill slots), not the IL — that's the CompCert split, and it keeps functional proofs stack-free.

2. **Non-inlinable calls.** Ctrl's `ret` returns to the boundary; there's no `call` with a saved return address. As long as LowIR is whole-program / fully-inlined, you never need it, and most str* functions are leaf, so this hasn't bitten. The moment you want *compositional* per-function verification with a real `call`, you need return-address saving across nesting → a stack, and then per-frame locals become natural.

3. **Address-taken locals and non-caller-provided buffers.** A register can't have its address taken. If some libc function does `&local` (passes a local by pointer) or needs a scratch buffer that isn't caller-supplied or static, that storage *must* be addressable → a stack slot.

**Recommendation.** Keep the IL register+flat-mem and stack-agnostic for now; push spilling and frame setup into `compile_sim` (forces 1 belongs there regardless). Introduce an explicit stack only when you hit the first function that needs forces 2 or 3 — and when you do, the clean way is a **designated `mem` region + an SP register convention, with each frame as a `Slice`** in the borrow layer you already built: a frame is a *unique* borrow, disjoint from the heap, from caller buffers, and from sibling frames. That makes "the callee's locals don't alias the caller's `&out` buffer" the *same* separation theorem as input/output disjointness in hex0 — no new machinery, and it answers the `sprintf(&errno,…)`-style aliasing worry uniformly. The choice of *when* is really the choice of call model: inline everything → defer the stack indefinitely; want IL-level `call` → add the frame/stack convention and locals-as-slots together.

One caveat worth flagging: deferring forces 1 to `compile_sim` means the Ctrl→RV64I compiler proof must include a verified register allocator (or a "no-spill if ≤N regs" side condition you discharge per program). That's real work, but it's the *one-time* cost we discussed — and keeping it out of the IL is what kept the hex0 functional proof clean.

### 👤 **User**  ·  03:53:35; waited 2m03s

But what if I want to have structs on the stack? If IL can’t represent it, it’s not possible to write such programs

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  03:54:40; model 1m26s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Let me push back on the premise a little, because I think the IL *can* already represent stack structs — the gap is narrower (and more interesting) than "can't represent it."

**Flat byte memory + arbitrary addressing already gives you stack structs.** `mem : Word → Byte` makes the *entire* address space addressable, and you can compute any address with `add`/`addi`. So a stack struct is just: pick a region (e.g. an `sp` register you decrement by the frame size), and read/write its fields with loads/stores at `sp + offset`. Its address is materializable in a register, so you can pass `&local` around. Nothing in the semantics forbids this — there's no enforcement of an SP convention, but there's no *obstacle* either. A struct-on-stack is representable today; it's just a memory region you manage by hand.

So what's *actually* missing is three things, in increasing order of significance:

**1. Load/store width (a real but small gap).** The IL only has `lbu` (byte load) and `sb` (byte store). Struct fields are usually 4/8 bytes. You can synthesize `lw`/`ld`/`sw`/`sd` from byte ops + shifts/or (hex0 already builds a byte from nibbles that way), but that's miserable for real structs. This is the one place I'd say "add to the IL" — `lw/lh/ld/sw/sh/sd` are trivial additive constructors with one-line `exec` equations, and they map directly to RV64I. This is genuine, do it when you need wider fields.

**2. Convention/sugar (not expressiveness).** No SP register, no `alloca`, no frame setup. You can do all of it by hand (`sp := sp - 16`), so it's not an enablement issue — it's ergonomics. You'd want a frame convention eventually, but it doesn't change what's *writable*.

**3. The real question — separation — and here's the genuine design fork.** With flat `Word → Byte`, when you place a struct at `sp - 16`, the IL gives you *no guarantee* that region doesn't alias the input buffer, the output buffer, the heap, errno, or another frame. To reason about the program you must *assert and maintain* that disjointness — exactly the `Slice`/`Wf` borrow discipline from hex0, now extended to "each stack frame is a unique borrow, disjoint from everything live." That works, but it's manual, and it scales linearly with the number of stack objects.

The heavyweight alternative is **block-structured memory** (CompCert's model): memory is `block_id → offset → byte`, and `alloca`/`malloc` return a *fresh* block. Freshness gives disjointness *for free* — a new frame can't alias any existing object because its block id is new, no proof obligation. That's a huge proof-engineering win for stack/heap-heavy code, and it matches C's UB model (no pointer arithmetic across objects). The cost: your memory is no longer flat, so address arithmetic and pointer comparison get more delicate, and `compile_sim` must prove the block model *refines* a flat RV64I address space (CompCert does exactly this — it's one of its substantial passes).

So the actual decision isn't "can the IL represent stack structs" (it can) — it's **which memory model you commit to**:

- **Flat `Word → Byte` + borrow discipline (your current path):** structs/stacks are just regions; separation is recovered *on top* via the `Slice`/`Wf`/Tree-Borrows layer you've started. Cheaper compile (memory is already the machine's), but every distinct object needs a maintained disjointness fact. Good while the number of live objects per proof is small (hex0: just input vs output).
- **Block-structured + `alloca`/`malloc` returning fresh blocks:** separation is automatic from freshness; far nicer for code with many stack frames and heap objects; but it's a bigger semantics change and an extra refinement obligation in `compile_sim`.

My read: stay flat, **add the wider load/stores now** (that's the only true expressiveness gap for structs), introduce the stack as `mem`-off-`sp` with each frame a unique `Slice`, and lean on the borrow layer for separation. If you reach a point where the manual disjointness bookkeeping for stack frames + heap dominates the proofs (it will, once you have non-leaf calls allocating frames), *that's* the signal to either add an `alloca`-style primitive that mints provably-fresh disjoint slices, or switch to a block memory model. The borrow layer is precisely the bet that you can defer (or avoid) that switch.

> 💭 (thinking)

Strongly agree, and the nice thing is hex0 already validates the endpoint of that pipeline — `hex0_correct`'s `Wf [⟨⟨inBase,len⟩,.shared⟩, ⟨⟨outBase,cap⟩,.uniq⟩]` precondition **is** the borrow signature for hex0, just *de-typed* into a `Prop` hypothesis. What you're describing is promoting that from "a side hypothesis the caller must re-prove" into "a fact the type system *produces*," carried in pointer types and lowered. That's the right move, and it's the standard separation-of-concerns: the type system at level N discharges what's an assumption at level N−1.

Here's how I'd frame the pipeline concretely, because each level wants a different representation of the same fact:

- **HIR (Tree Borrows, temporal):** `&'a [u8]` / `&'a mut [u8]` — pointer carries provenance + lifetime, validity is *time-varying* (reborrow, reservation→activation). Aliasing rules are TB's.
- **MIR (regions, spatial):** lifetimes erased; pointer carries a *region* (slice) and the context carries disjointness obligations. The temporal discipline has been *flattened* to "for the extent of this scope, these regions are disjoint."
- **LowIR (flat `Word→Byte`):** region = `Slice`, disjointness = the `Wf`/`Disjoint` hypotheses on the functional theorem. Exactly hex0's `hdisj`.

So "what the pointer is valid for + disjoint with" travels as: **type index (HIR) → region annotation + `Wf` side-condition (MIR) → `Slice` + `Disjoint` hypothesis (LowIR)**, and each lowering pass has a translation-correctness theorem that *transports the guarantee into the lower vocabulary*. The borrow layer we built is the LowIR-level word for what the HIR types mean.

One subtlety worth being explicit about, because it's the crux of "translates into disjoint regions": **TB is temporal; spatial disjointness is its function-boundary summary.** TB's whole reason to exist is that legality changes over time within a body. When you lower to "these regions are disjoint for this call," you're projecting that temporal discipline onto the call's duration. That's *lossy* but *sound and sufficient* for a callee that doesn't itself do fancy reborrowing — which is exactly why hex0's purely-spatial `Wf` was enough. So the design is: keep full TB at the HIR (for bodies that need it), but the **function contract** lowers to the spatial summary, which is precisely what each callee's proof consumes. Don't try to carry TB's temporal state into LowIR — carry its spatial shadow.

On "this should be in the type signature" — yes, and there's a choice of how much machinery:

1. **Refinement-as-`Prop` (today):** explicit slice args + a `Wf` precondition. It's a *contract* but not a *type*; the caller re-proves `Wf` at every call site, and nothing stops you forming an out-of-bounds access. This is what hex0 has.
2. **Indexed pointers (the sweet spot, I think):** `Ptr (s : Slice) (perm : Perm)` — the *region is in the pointer's type*, disjointness is a value/relation in a "live-borrows" context. Now a load must carry a within-slice proof (memory safety falls out), and two `.uniq` borrows in context are disjoint by the context's `Wf` invariant. Lowering erases the index, emits the `Disjoint` facts.
3. **Full borrow type system, soundness proved once (RustBelt/λRust style):** a typing judgment whose *soundness theorem* says well-typed ⇒ the operational separation invariants hold. Then signatures literally *are* borrow types, and the checker *produces* the `Wf`/`Disjoint` facts — you never hand-prove them per function.

(3) is the "pay once" dream — and it's the same economics as `compile_sim`: prove the type system sound once, every program's borrow obligations are discharged by typing. But it's a large effort (RustBelt is a research project). For a bootstrap libc I'd build **(2) with a small, once-proved soundness lemma**: a handful of borrow-formation/sub-borrow rules, proved sound against the flat-memory semantics, so that `Disjoint`/in-bounds become *consequences of well-typedness* rather than per-call hypotheses — without committing to a full lifetime calculus up front. You can grow toward (3) if/when reborrowing inside bodies forces it.

Net: your instinct is right and it's *not* block memory in disguise — block memory puts separation in the **memory model** (freshness); you're putting it in the **type system / signatures** and threading it through lowering as data+proofs, keeping the bottom flat and machine-faithful. hex0's `Wf` precondition is the first concrete instance; the work is (a) make the slice a pointer *index* not a loose arg, and (b) prove the borrow-formation rules sound *once* so signatures generate the disjointness instead of demanding it.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:28:23; model 12.4s

Done. Wrote `docs/LOWIR-DESIGN.md` and linked it in the docs index.

It records, as a decision log:

**Decisions made (each with rationale + alternatives + caveat):**
- **D1** Structured IL vs direct bare-RISC-V — abstracts PC/encoding, amortizes via `compile_sim`; caveat: relocates machine complexity rather than removing it.
- **D2** Unbounded register file + total flat byte memory — vs bounded-32/block-structured; caveat: the infinite-register fiction makes spilling a `compile_sim` obligation.
- **D3** Non-local control flow (`ret`/`block`/`brk`/`cont`) in `Ctrl` — flat ret-cascade error handling; caveat: paid back in the unwritten control-flow-lowering compiler, and pure overhead for straight-line loops (the strlen finding).
- **D4** Clocked big-step (fuel) vs relational/small-step — executable + structural; caveat: needed `exec_mono` to tame fuel composition.
- **D5** Borrows-on-flat-memory vs block-structured memory — the separation lives in types/contracts, not the memory model.
- **D6** Single-threaded ⇒ `errno` is a plain global, not TLS.

**Deliberate non-features:** no stack/SP/frames/`alloca`, no `call`/return-address, byte-only load/store, no register-count limit, manual separation, no `Ctrl` `compile_sim`.

**Likely extensions, ordered by when they'll be forced:** wider load/store (the one true expressiveness gap for structs) → stack-as-`mem`-region+SP with frames as unique slices → `call`/return for compositional verification → `alloca`/fresh-region primitive (local "freshness ⇒ disjointness") → the borrow-typed higher-IR pipeline (HIR/TB-temporal → MIR/regions-spatial → LowIR/`Wf`-hypotheses, with the three rigor levels and the recommended indexed-pointer sweet spot) → `Ctrl` `compile_sim` → toolbox factoring.

And §4 states the thesis in one line: separation lives in the type system / function contracts and is threaded down as data+proofs to `Wf`/`Disjoint` hypotheses on flat-memory theorems — keep LowIR flat and machine-faithful, pay for the compiler once, let the future borrow type system *produce* the disjointness LowIR proofs assume.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:29:50; model 1m41s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

The key reframe: **nothing changes the meaning of a single global invariant.** What changes over time is *which resources a given context owns* — and that's modeled by ownership **transfer** plus a **frame rule**, not by a mutating predicate. "Borrows change meaning during execution" really means "ownership of a region moves between scopes/functions, and is reclaimed when a lifetime ends." There are two altitudes at which you can make that precise, and the IR uses different ones at different levels.

## Two ways to represent the temporal part

**(a) As state (Tree Borrows operationally).** The per-location borrow tree is *part of the machine state* and steps as accesses happen (Reserved→Active→Frozen→Disabled, child tags on reborrow). Here "validity changes over time" is just the state evolving — faithful, but heavy; you only want this at the top IR, and only if you interpret borrows operationally (Miri-style).

**(b) As threaded resources (separation logic / the RustBelt reading).** You never hold one invariant that mutates; you hold *ownership assertions* (`ℓ ↦ v`, permission-indexed) that **move**. A `&'a mut s` is a *time-bounded* resource: you can access `s` while lifetime `'a` is alive (you hold a lifetime token), and when `'a` ends the full ownership of `s` is returned to whoever lent it. Reborrowing = *splitting* a resource into a child borrow for a sub-scope, with the parent **frozen** until the child's lifetime ends, then merged back. The "meaning changing over time" is entirely captured by *when you own what* — and the **frame rule** keeps everything you didn't mention stable, automatically.

The LowIR design deliberately uses neither in full: it only admits borrows **held for the whole function call**, which is the temporal discipline collapsed to a single scope. That's the "spatial shadow." Genuine intra-body reborrowing (validity flipping mid-body) is *not* expressible against LowIR's static `Wf` — it must live at an IR that has the TB state or the ownership logic, and be *lowered away* (to disjoint slices + copies) before reaching LowIR.

## How calls propagate the invariants

Not via a shared mutable invariant — via a **function spec = Hoare pre/post over borrows + ownership transfer + frame**:

1. **Precondition** = the borrows the callee requires, with their permissions *and their mutual disjointness*. (`hex0`'s `Wf [shared s_in, unique s_out]` is exactly this, in degenerate call-free form.)
2. **The call transfers** those resources from caller to callee for the call's duration. Crucially, the caller **loses access to the unique borrows it lent** — that's how `&mut` exclusivity is enforced *across* a call, temporally: while the callee runs, the caller provably can't touch `s_out`.
3. **Postcondition** = the resources handed back (possibly transformed): "on return, `s_out` holds the decoded bytes; ownership of `s_in` and `s_out` returns to you."
4. **Frame rule** = everything the callee's precondition *didn't* mention is untouched and stays owned/valid in the caller. This is what makes the propagation compositional: the caller does **not** re-prove that the callee avoids its other data — framing gives it for free.

So the split is clean: **disjointness among the callee's *arguments* is the precondition** (the caller establishes it; at a higher IR the borrow checker *produces* it); **disjointness from the caller's *other* memory is the frame** (free). That's the whole "how do calls propagate it" answer.

## hex0 already prefigures the frame

This isn't hypothetical for this project. `main_loop` maintains, as part of its invariant, that the **input region is unchanged** while only the output slice is written. That *is* the frame condition for the unique output borrow: the borrow's footprint is `s_out`, and everything outside it (the input, and by extension any caller memory) is preserved. So a future *caller* of hex0 reasons exactly as above: "I lent `s_out` unique and `s_in` shared; on return `s_out` is filled, `s_in` and all my other memory are unchanged." The pieces to turn `hex0_correct` into a framed, callable spec are already in the proof — you'd repackage "input unchanged + Disjoint" as "`mem` outside `s_out` is framed."

## Where each piece of temporality lands in the pipeline

- **HIR (TB):** borrows are types with lifetimes; temporal validity is the borrow-tree state (or RustBelt-style semantic typing). At a call, the type system checks you pass what the signature wants, you haven't used a lent-out `&mut`, and lifetimes nest. *This is where intra-body reborrowing is allowed and checked.*
- **Lowering:** lifetimes are erased; the facts the type system *proved* (unique-disjoint, shared-overlap-ok, in-bounds, held-for-the-call) are **emitted as the callee's precondition** + the frame. The temporal evolution is *discharged by typing upstairs*; only the per-call static residue survives.
- **LowIR:** sees only the static per-function contract — `Wf`/`Disjoint`/in-bounds pre, mutated-footprint + frame post. No temporality, because the higher IR guaranteed the borrows are stable for the call's duration.

## The honest limitation

LowIR's `Wf`-as-`Prop` models *borrows-for-the-whole-call* only. The moment you want either (i) calls, or (ii) intra-body reborrowing, the static `Prop` is no longer enough on its own — you need the **ownership/resource reading with a frame rule** at the IR where calls/reborrows occur. For calls specifically, that means upgrading LowIR function specs from "`Wf` → result" to a separation-style **{pre} f {post} with framing**, where the pre/post are over borrowed slices and the frame is "memory outside the unique footprints is preserved." That's the single most important extension once `call` lands — and hex0's "input unchanged" invariant shows the frame is already within reach.

Want me to fold this — the ownership-transfer + frame-rule call model, and the "borrows are threaded resources, not a mutating invariant" framing — into `LOWIR-DESIGN.md` (extension 3, `call`/return) so it's recorded with the rest?

```
86	    | .while c a b body =>

94	          | none                   => none
95	        else some (s, .normal)
96	    | .brkB k          => some (s, .brk k)
```

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:42:00; model 20.1s

Done — `call` is now in the IL and there's a sorry-free worked example. Here's what it demonstrates, which is exactly your two requirements.

## The `call` construct (`lean/LowIR/Ctrl.lean`)

```lean
| call (g : Stmt)          -- runs the callee body; catches its `ret`
```
```lean
| .call g => match exec fuel g s with
             | some (s', .ret) => some (s', .normal)   -- callee returned → caller continues
             | other           => other
```

It's a **real function-return boundary**, not inlining: the callee's `ret` is caught and becomes the caller's `.normal` (the caller resumes), exactly like `run` but as a statement. `exec_mono` is extended to cover it, and there are one-layer `exec_call_{normal,ret,brk,cont,none}` lemmas. The existing hex0/strtoull proofs are unaffected — the change is purely additive.

## The example (`lean/LowIR/CtrlCall.lean`)

A leaf callee and a caller that calls it, designed so disjointness *across* the call is essential:

```lean
def loadByteFn   : Stmt := .lbu 12 10 0                     -- x12 := mem[x10]   (the callee)
def writeThenLoad : Stmt := .seq (.sb 11 13 0) (.call loadByteFn)  -- mem[x11] := x13; then call
```

```lean
theorem writeThenLoad_spec (s) (src dst : Word)
    (h10 : s.rget 10 = src) (h11 : s.rget 11 = dst)
    (hdisj : Disjoint ⟨src, 1⟩ ⟨dst, 1⟩) :
    ∃ st', exec 3 writeThenLoad s = some (st', .normal)
      ∧ st'.rget 12 = (s.mem src).setWidth 64    -- loaded the ORIGINAL src byte
      ∧ st'.mem src = s.mem src                  -- src preserved
```

The proof does three things you asked for:

- **Calls are present and used modularly.** At the call site the caller invokes `loadByteFn_spec` (the callee's spec: "reads `mem[x10]`, frame = memory untouched") via `exec_call_normal` — it **never unfolds the callee's body**. That's compositional verification: prove the callee once, call sites use the spec.
- **Disjointness *across* the call.** The caller writes `dst` *before* calling the callee that reads `src`. For the loaded value to be the original `src` byte, the write mustn't have disturbed it — discharged by `storeByte_preserves` under the `Disjoint ⟨src,1⟩ ⟨dst,1⟩` precondition. This is precisely "the caller's write footprint must be disjoint from the callee's read footprint," propagated as a borrow precondition.
- **Within-function disjointness is the same mechanism.** The `sb` (write to `dst`) and the callee's `lbu` (read of `src`) are two footprints in one function body; the same `Disjoint`/`storeByte_preserves` discharges their non-interference — there's no separate machinery for "within" vs "across."

The frame is also visible: `st'.mem src = s.mem src` plus the callee's "memory untouched" spec means everything outside the write footprint survives the call.

**Caveat (honest scope):** this is *call-by-body* (`.call` carries the callee `Stmt`). For a real libc with a function table you'd index calls by name with an environment, and the modular-spec reasoning is identical — that's a straightforward generalization. And lowering `.call` to RV64I (`jal`/`ra`/stack frame) is part of the still-unwritten `Ctrl` `compile_sim`. But at the IL/functional level, calls now exist and cross-call disjointness reasoning works, sorry-free.

Want me to record this `call` design (semantics + the modular/frame reasoning pattern) into `LOWIR-DESIGN.md` under extension 3?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:43:55; model 1m59s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

You're right that the calling convention is real and that *somewhere* it forces an explicit stack. The key move is to see that the convention splits into two separable things, and they live at different levels:

1. **The contract** — which registers survive a call (the caller/callee agreement). This is a *spec-level* fact.
2. **The enforcement** — the save/restore code that makes the contract true. *This* is what needs the stack.

## (1) belongs in the IL spec; (2) belongs in the lowering

At the **IL functional layer** (`LowIR.Ctrl`), only the contract matters, and it's already expressible: a function's spec states its footprint — which registers/memory it reads, writes, and *preserves*. The demo's `loadByteFn_spec` is exactly this ("writes `x12`, leaves memory untouched"); a caller reads the preserved-set off the spec and composes. **No stack, no ABI, no save/restore at this level** — and that's deliberate, because it's what keeps `hex0_correct` and friends convention-agnostic and clean. The IL says *what* survives a call; it doesn't say *how*.

The **enforcement** is compiler-synthesized at the **IL→machine lowering** (`compile_sim`), parameterized by the platform ABI:

- Register allocation maps the IL's ∞ registers onto the 32 physical ones (the obligation that already exists because `Reg = Nat`).
- A value that must be **live across a call** is either placed in a **callee-saved** physical register (so the callee's prologue/epilogue saves+restores it) or **spilled by the caller** to its frame around the call. Which physical regs are callee- vs caller-saved is the *ABI parameter*.
- `ra` is saved/restored by any non-leaf callee (its own `call`s clobber it).
- **All of this save/restore lands in the stack frame** — which is where the explicit stack enters. So: the stack appears at the IR/pass that *commits to physical registers + ABI*, not in `LowIR.Ctrl`.

"Different platforms need different registers saved" = **the ABI is a parameter to `compile_sim`, not to the IL**:

```lean
structure ABI where
  sp ra      : PReg
  argRegs    : List PReg          -- a0…  (caller-saved)
  retReg     : PReg
  calleeSaved : List PReg         -- RISC-V: s0–s11 ; SysV x86-64: rbx,rbp,r12–r15 ; AArch64: x19–x28
  callerSaved : List PReg         -- temporaries / arg regs
```

The IL theorem `exec p s ≡ spec` is **platform-independent and proved once**; `compile_sim` is instantiated per ABI, and the per-platform save/restore discipline is verified there. The correctness statement is what nails the convention down precisely:

> For ABI `A`: if `exec p s = some (s', o)` in the IL, then the compiled machine code, run from a machine state matching `s` with `sp` pointing at a fresh frame region **disjoint from the program's data**, reaches a state matching `s'`, **with `A.calleeSaved` and `ra` restored to their entry values**, and the frame region is the only extra memory touched.

That "callee-saved restored" clause *is* the calling convention, and it's exactly what makes a caller's IL-level claim "`x_i` is preserved across this call" *true* on the machine — because the allocator put `x_i` in a callee-saved physical register (or the caller spilled it). The IL's preserved-set claims become **proof obligations discharged by the verified register allocator + spiller** — which, as flagged earlier, is the genuinely hard part of `compile_sim`.

## So where does the stack get "explicitly mentioned"?

At a **machine-level IR (or directly in `compile_sim`) that has `sp` + frames** — introduced by the convention-establishing lowering. CompCert does exactly this: front-end IRs (Cminor/RTL) are convention-agnostic; the calling convention, register allocation, spilling, and the concrete frame (saved regs, `ra`, spill slots) are introduced in the back-end (Linear→Mach→Asm) and proved there. And conveniently, **that same frame holds the address-taken locals / stack structs** from our earlier discussion — so the stack shows up *once*, at this lowering, serving both the ABI save/restore and program-level stack data.

The alternative — putting `sp`/frames/prologue-epilogue into `LowIR.Ctrl` itself and *writing* the convention there — is a flatter design (fewer IRs) but mixes concerns: your functional proofs (`hex0_correct`) would then have to step over frame setup and ABI plumbing. I'd keep `LowIR.Ctrl` convention-agnostic and push the stack into the lowering, but it's a real choice and you've been leaning explicit, so it's yours to make.

**Net:** the contract (preserved-set) is already in the IL via function specs and composes across calls today; the enforcement (save/restore) + the stack are introduced by the per-ABI `compile_sim` lowering, with the ABI as a parameter and "callee-saved restored" as the formal convention guarantee. The IL stays platform-independent; the stack is mentioned exactly where physical registers and the ABI are.

Want me to (a) record this — the contract-vs-enforcement split, the `ABI`-parameterized `compile_sim`, and "stack enters at the convention lowering" — into `LOWIR-DESIGN.md` (it slots into extensions 3/6), and/or (b) prototype the small `ABI` record + the machine-IR-with-`sp` skeleton so the lowering obligation is written down concretely?
