does llvm IR mention stack explicitly?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:44:55; model 53.6s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Partly — and the way LLVM splits it is almost exactly the design I was recommending, so it's a good reference point.

**What LLVM IR *does* mention explicitly:**

- **`alloca`** — yes, a first-class instruction that allocates memory "on the stack frame of the currently executing function," returns a pointer, and is auto-freed on return. This is precisely the *address-taken-locals / stack-structs* category (the "(b)" from earlier): if you take `&local`, have an array/struct local, or need a scratch buffer, you `alloca` it and get a `ptr`. So **stack *allocation* is explicit and abstract** — you say "I want N bytes of frame," not where in the frame or how `sp` moves.
- A few **introspection/scoping hooks**: `llvm.stacksave`/`llvm.stackrestore` (for VLA / dynamic-alloca scopes), `llvm.frameaddress`, `llvm.returnaddress`. Limited, not used by ordinary codegen.

**What LLVM IR does *not* mention — and pushes to the backend:**

- **No physical registers** — infinite SSA virtual values, no 32-register file.
- **No stack pointer, no frame layout, no prologue/epilogue, no spill code, no `ra` save, no callee-saved/caller-saved distinction.** All of that is synthesized by CodeGen: register allocation invents spill slots, `PrologEpilogInserter` emits the frame setup and callee-saved save/restore, the SP arithmetic appears only in MIR/MC.
- **The calling convention is an *attribute/tag*** (`ccc`, `fastcc`, `cc <n>`, plus param attributes like `byval`, `sret`, `inreg`) on functions and `call` sites — a *label that directs the backend's lowering*, not executable IR. The IR `call` just names callee + args.

So LLVM draws the line exactly where I suggested for your IL: **explicit `alloca`-style stack *allocation* for program-visible locals; the ABI save/restore + the physical `sp`/frame stay in the lowering, with the convention as a parameter (an attribute).** `alloca` ≠ the calling-convention stack — they're different concerns, and only the first is in the IR.

Two more LLVM details that bear directly on your earlier instincts:

- **Disjointness *is* in LLVM signatures** — via the `noalias` parameter attribute (the IR-level reflection of C `restrict`), plus `dereferenceable(n)`, `align`, `readonly`/`writeonly`. That's your "pointer carries what it's valid for and that it's disjoint from others, in the type signature" idea, already shipping: `noalias` on a `ptr` param is "this doesn't alias other accessible memory." Your `Slice`/`Wf`/borrow layer is a *more precise, proof-carrying* version of the same thing (LLVM's `noalias` is an unchecked promise the optimizer trusts; yours is a verified hypothesis).
- **LLVM memory is *not* a flat byte array** at the IR level — it has **provenance** (objects from `alloca`/globals/`malloc` are distinct; `getelementptr inbounds`, pointer-comparison and aliasing rules depend on it). So LLVM sits between flat-bytes and block-structured: pointers carry provenance precisely so alias analysis (and `noalias`) is sound. That's the same tension you resolved by choosing flat-memory-plus-borrows: LLVM chose provenance-in-the-pointer; you chose disjointness-in-the-contract.

**Takeaway for your IL:** following LLVM, add an explicit `alloca`/frame-allocation construct to `LowIR.Ctrl` when you need address-taken locals/structs (program-visible stack), but keep the calling-convention `sp`/save/restore in `compile_sim` with the ABI as a parameter — and consider promoting your `Slice`/`Disjoint` precondition into a `noalias`-style *pointer/param attribute* at the higher IR, which is the typed form you were reaching for.

### 👤 **User**  ·  18:46:53; waited 1m41s

any good design decisions to copy from CompCert/CakeML?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  18:48:02; model 1m35s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

A handful, and several you're already aligned with. Let me give the ones with real payoff for *this* project (verified IL → RV64I for a bootstrap libc), tied to where you are.

## From CompCert

**1. Many small passes, each with a `match_states` forward simulation, composed by transitivity.** CompCert is ~11 languages (Clight→…→Cminor→RTL→LTL→Linear→Mach→Asm), each pass proved semantics-preserving via an explicit source↔target state relation, then chained. **Don't make `compile_sim` a monolith** — it's really *four very different beasts*: control-flow lowering (`while/block/ret`→jumps+labels), register allocation+spilling, frame/ABI insertion, and instruction encoding. Make each a separate pass with its own `match_states`, compose by transitivity. This is the single highest-value structural decision to copy, and it directly de-risks the hard `Ctrl` compiler.

**2. One memory model shared across *all* IR levels.** CompCert uses the same `Mem` from Clight down to Asm, so memory facts transport without per-pass translation. You chose flat-bytes+borrows instead of their blocks — fine — but copy the *discipline*: keep `St`/`mem` (and the `Slice`/`Disjoint` layer) identical across `LowIR.Ctrl` and the lower machine IR, so disjointness facts carry down unchanged. CompCert also attaches **per-byte permissions** (Freeable/Writable/Readable) to memory — a coarse, built-in capability notion; your borrow layer is a sharper, proof-carrying version of the same idea, so you're on a well-trodden path.

**3. External/unverified functions as an axiomatized spec interface with built-in frame properties** (`external_call`). CompCert models syscalls/unknown functions by a predicate that's guaranteed to (among other things) **not touch memory outside its footprint** — the frame rule, axiomatized. For a *bootstrap* libc this is exactly the boundary you want: verify the functions you've reached, and give the rest (and syscalls) a pre/post+frame spec. It also makes your `.call` story uniform — a call uses the callee's spec whether the callee is verified or axiomatized.

**4. Preserve *observable behavior* (event traces), not just final state.** CompCert's theorems are about traces of external events (I/O), not registers+memory. The moment libc hits `read`/`write`/`printf`, "correct final memory" isn't enough — you need the syscall trace. **Add an event/trace output to `exec` now** (cheap) rather than retrofit it after you've built more proofs on the state-only semantics.

## From CakeML

**5. The clocked functional big-step you already use is *their* validated choice.** CakeML's semantics (Owens et al., "functional big-step semantics") is clocked, executable, total, and proven equivalent to small-step. So your `exec : Nat → …` plus `exec_mono` isn't a workaround — it's the endorsed style, and the monotonicity/determinism lemmas are the known, one-time cost. Keep it; you're in good company.

**6. Introduce the explicit stack *low*, in a dedicated IR** (their `stackLang`, below register-abstract `wordLang`). CakeML keeps registers abstract high up and only makes the stack — stack pointer, register save/restore — explicit in `stackLang`→`labLang`→bytes. This is direct confirmation of the answer to your last two questions: keep `LowIR.Ctrl` register-abstract and stack-free; add a `stackLang`-like machine IR where the ABI save/restore and `sp`/frame live. Copy the *layering*, not just the conclusion.

**7. Verify all the way to the actual bytes, including the encoder.** CakeML proves down to machine code for several ISAs (RISC-V among them) with a verified assembler/`encode`. For you the *bytes are the deliverable* (hex0 bootstrap), so the `encode` proof is not optional — it's the point. CompCert historically stopped nearer assembly; CakeML's all-the-way rigor is the one to match here.

**8. Target-parametric bottom: an abstract `asm` + per-ISA encoder/config.** CakeML retargets (x86/ARM/RISC-V/…) by instantiating one abstract assembly with an encoder + small config. Even if you only want RV64I now, structure the bottom layer so the **ISA and the ABI are parameters** (this is your "ABI-as-parameter" idea generalized). Cheap insurance for retargeting, and it makes the calling-convention parameterization natural.

**9. Separation-logic program logic for modular reasoning** (CakeML's characteristic formulae / CF). CakeML reasons about programs modularly with Hoare triples + a frame rule. That's exactly where your borrow/disjointness + frame discussion landed — so the function-spec-with-frame discipline is the validated mechanism, not an improvisation.

## What to *not* copy (or adapt, not adopt)

- **CompCert's block memory model** — you consciously rejected it for flat+borrows; that's a legitimate, different point in the design space (provenance-in-contracts vs provenance-in-pointers). Just keep the *shared-model* discipline (#2).
- **CompCert's trusted C frontend / preprocessor and its unverified glue** — for a bootstrap whose whole purpose is shrinking the TCB, minimize trusted frontends; write libc functions directly in the IL (as you're doing) rather than trusting a C parser.
- **CakeML's GC/heap value representation** — irrelevant to C-style libc.

**If I had to pick three for the next move:** (1) factor `compile_sim` into composed passes with `match_states`; (6) the low explicit-stack IR for the ABI; and (4) add an event trace now so I/O-bearing libc functions are even *statable*. Want me to fold this — especially the multi-pass/`match_states` decomposition and the `stackLang`-style stack IR — into `LOWIR-DESIGN.md` as the recommended compiler-side architecture?

### 👤 **User**  ·  01:57:01; waited 31h04m

how does shared/unique constraints on pointers compare to llvm provenance?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  01:58:38; model 2m03s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

They overlap in *purpose* — both are about when you're allowed to assume two pointers don't interfere — but they're different *kinds* of object, and decomposing a borrow shows exactly where they line up and diverge.

A borrow ≈ **region** (`Slice`: "what it's valid for") + **permission** (shared/unique) + **lifetime**. Map those onto LLVM:

- **region ↔ provenance.** LLVM provenance is the identity of the *allocation/object* a pointer was derived from; an access is well-defined only within that object (out-of-bounds GEP/access → poison/UB). Your `Slice` plays the same "which bytes may this pointer touch" role and gives the same spatial-safety guarantee (accesses must be in-slice ⇒ bounds-safe). This is the part that genuinely corresponds.
- **permission (shared/unique) ↔ `noalias`/`restrict`, *not* provenance.** Provenance has **no read/write-exclusivity axis**. Two LLVM pointers with the *same* provenance can both be live and one can write — totally fine; provenance says nothing. Exclusivity is a *separate, layered* promise: `noalias` (the IR reflection of C `restrict`) means "accesses via this pointer don't alias accesses via others, in this scope." So your `unique` ≈ `noalias`, your `shared` ≈ `readonly`+overlap-allowed — and these are bolted *on top of* provenance in LLVM, whereas in your design shared/unique is first-class.
- **lifetime ↔ allocation lifetime, but much coarser.** Provenance has a notion of "this object is dead now" (after `free`/return → dangling), but it has nothing like Tree Borrows' *temporal* lifecycle (reborrow, reservation→activation, parent frozen while child is live). Borrows are richly temporal; provenance is essentially static identity + alive/dead.

Then the sharper, structural differences:

**Ownership/affine vs freely-copyable value.** This is the big one. A `unique` borrow is *substructural* — you can't duplicate it (you move or reborrow it); uniqueness is enforced by *the capability not being aliasable*. An LLVM pointer is an ordinary SSA value: **copy it as many times as you like**; provenance just rides along on every copy. The constraint is purely on *accesses*, never on *duplication of the pointer*. So borrows restrict aliasing of the *capability itself* (ownership typing); provenance restricts only what an *access* may touch. That's why Rust can give `&mut` exclusivity structurally and C/LLVM needs `restrict` as an out-of-band promise.

**Failure mode / direction of trust.** Violating a borrow (two overlapping `unique`s, writing through `shared`) is **ill-formed**, and the discipline *produces* separation you then rely on. Violating provenance (accessing outside the object, or aliasing across `noalias`) is **UB/poison**, and the discipline is an **assumption the optimizer trusts** — the frontend must avoid the UB, and if it does, the optimizer exploits it. Borrows are a *proved contract* (or to-be-checked by a borrow type system); provenance is *trusted, largely unverified in practice* — which is why it's been a multi-year soundness saga (the `inttoptr`/`ptrtoint` ↔ optimizer interaction, PNVI, the Lee/Hur/Kang "reconciling high-level optimizations and low-level code" line). The hard part of provenance is keeping it sound *through integer↔pointer casts and address arithmetic*.

**The deep relation:** Tree Borrows *is* Rust's provenance. TB and the LLVM/C provenance models are siblings answering the same question — "when may the compiler assume non-aliasing" — for different source languages, with Rust's type system *restricting programs* enough to make the discipline checkable (Miri), whereas C must cope with arbitrary pointer/integer punning, which is why its provenance is messier.

**For your project specifically** — the interesting part — **your pointers have no provenance at all.** An address is a flat `Word`; disjointness lives entirely in the `Wf`/`Disjoint` *contract*, not in the pointer value. Two consequences:

1. You **sidestep LLVM's hardest problem**: there's no provenance to preserve through `ptrtoint`/`inttoptr` or address arithmetic, so the entire int↔ptr-cast quagmire that makes LLVM provenance contentious simply doesn't arise. An address that equals another address *is* the same address; whether accesses interfere is a separate, *proved* fact.
2. You get **soundly** what provenance gives *heuristically*. LLVM provenance exists so the optimizer can *assume* non-aliasing to justify a transformation, on faith the program has no UB. Your borrow contract lets a transformation assume non-aliasing because it's *proved* — same payoff, no trust gap. The price is symmetric: LLVM's non-aliasing is automatic-but-unsound-in-adversarial-C; yours is sound-but-manual (you must supply/maintain the `Disjoint` precondition, or have a borrow type system produce it).

So if you ever lower through an LLVM-like layer, the natural mapping is: `unique` → `noalias` + `dereferenceable(len)`, `shared` → `readonly` + `dereferenceable(len)`, region → the provenance/object — and your *verified contract is the ground truth that makes those attributes actually sound*, rather than promises the optimizer takes on faith. That inversion — provenance-as-trusted-assumption vs borrow-as-proved-contract — is the cleanest one-line summary of the difference.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  02:02:36; model 1m49s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Good instinct — and it has a precise lineage: `Word → Option (BorrowState × Byte)` is essentially **CompCert-style permissioned memory** / a **fractional-permission heap** (Boyland) / a coarse **operational Tree Borrows** (what Miri runs). The two halves of your proposal are actually quite different in character, though, and I'd treat them separately.

**The `Option` half (partial memory = validity/allocation) is the clean, separable win.** `Word → Option Byte` gives you a *memory-safety* notion intrinsic to `exec`: a load/store at `none` is UB/error, so "valid pointer" becomes a fact the semantics checks rather than an external assumption. That's exactly CompCert's choice (its `Mem` is partial-with-permissions), and it's orthogonal to borrows. It does cost something at `compile_sim` — the real RV64I memory is *total* (every address reads something), so you'd prove the program only touches backed addresses (the spatial-safety theorem you want anyway) and the bytes agree. Reasonable to adopt on its own.

**The `BorrowState` half is where I'd push back — it inverts the design decision and pays at every level.** Recall we deliberately put separation in *contracts* (borrow-as-`Prop`) over a flat machine-matching memory. Fusing borrow-state into operational `mem` undoes that, with three concrete costs:

1. **It's ghost/instrumentation, but you'd be storing it as real state.** The RV64I machine stores bytes, not `(borrowstate, byte)` pairs. So `BorrowState` is instrumentation that must be *erased* at the bottom — `compile_sim` now owes a tag-erasure refinement (tagged memory refines flat bytes, tags don't affect observable behavior). That's a substantial extra obligation, landing back in the compiler proof.
2. **Every functional proof gets heavier.** With borrow-state in `exec`, each load/store checks and mutates tags, so `hex0_correct` would have to thread the borrow-state invariant *through the whole loop induction*. The contract approach kept borrow reasoning at the *boundary* (one `Wf` precondition) and entirely out of `main_loop`. Fusing it in is the opposite of the "residue, not the operational model" decision in `MEMORY-BORROWS.md`.
3. **It drags temporal complexity down to the wrong level.** `BorrowState` transitions (Reserved→Active→Frozen…) are the temporal TB machinery we said to keep at higher IRs and *flatten* to the per-call spatial shadow for LowIR. Per-cell state brings the automaton back down.

There's also a representational ceiling: **a single per-byte state can't express full Tree Borrows.** TB is per-location *per-tag* — a *tree* of borrows at each address — precisely so it can distinguish several live borrows of the same location. One `BorrowState` per cell collapses that to a per-location summary, which can only model a coarse own/share/free (≈ fractional) discipline — weaker than TB. So you'd get something CompCert-permission-like, not TB.

And the fractional reading exposes why it wants to be logical, not operational: shared/unique map beautifully to fractions (unique = full `1`, shared = `<1`, split `p = p/2 + p/2` to share, recombine to regain write). But the fraction isn't a single value living *in the cell* — it's *distributed across the assertions of the various holders*. That splitting/recombining is exactly what a separation logic tracks in its resources; a lone per-cell field can't compose it. Which is why **RustBelt/Iris keep the heap plain (`Word ⇀ Byte`) and put borrows/fractions in the *logic* as ghost resources** — the principled realization of "separation in the contracts, not in the operational memory," i.e. the choice you already made, just with more machinery.

So my recommendation:

- **Take the `Option`** if you want memory-safety in the model: `mem : Word → Option Byte`. Useful, orthogonal, CompCert-blessed.
- **Keep borrow/permission state out of operational `mem`** — represent shared/unique as *ghost/logical* resources over plain bytes (your `Slice`/`Wf` contract is already a coarse, per-region version of fractional permissions; the full version is Iris-style ghost state). This keeps functional proofs boundary-only and keeps `compile_sim` from owing a tag-erasure refinement.
- **If you specifically want to *run* programs and catch aliasing UB** (Miri-style), build `Word → Option (BorrowState × Byte)` as a **separate instrumented semantics** for validation/testing, with a theorem that it refines the plain semantics — but don't make it the semantics your correctness proofs and compiler run on.

Tie-back to the provenance question: putting state in the *cell* (this proposal) is the memory-side dual of LLVM putting provenance in the *pointer*. Your contract approach puts it in *neither* operational place and keeps it in the proof/logic — which is what dodges both the provenance-through-int-casts mess *and* the tag-erasure refinement. Fusing borrow-state into `mem` trades that clean position for an executable checker you mostly don't need at the proof/compile level.

IMO, the only sane way to reason about provenance is treating pointer values as magically storing provenance in "parallel" RAM, which gets destroyed if you store anything but a pointer. this is what FilC does

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  02:22:29; model 1m42s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Yes — and that model has a name and a hardware embodiment: it's **CHERI**, and FilC is essentially its software realization. CHERI memory is exactly "parallel RAM": one **tag bit per capability-sized word**, maintained by the hardware alongside the data bits, and **cleared automatically whenever you store non-capability data** over that location. FilC's *InvisiCaps* do the same in software — a pointer's capability (bounds + provenance) is tracked out-of-band, keyed to the storage location, and a non-pointer store destroys it. So your "magically storing provenance in parallel RAM, destroyed if you store anything but a pointer" is precisely the CHERI/FilC capability model.

I agree it's the sane *operational* model, and it's worth being explicit about *why*, because the "why" is the soundness theorem:

- Provenance must **not live in the integer/address bits** — that's the broken approach: forgeable by arithmetic, undefined under `ptrtoint`/`inttoptr`, and the source of LLVM's entire provenance saga. Keep the integer a plain integer with *no* provenance (this is the PNVI — "provenance not via integers" — principle the C memory-model TR landed on).
- Put provenance in a **location-keyed shadow**, and make it **monotone / unforgeable**: you can only obtain a capability legitimately (allocation, in-bounds derivation), never synthesize one from data. The **clear-on-non-pointer-store rule is exactly what enforces unforgeability** — it's not incidental, it's the crux. CHERI's formal models prove their headline guarantee (capability monotonicity / no provenance forgery) precisely from that invariant. So your rule *is* the theorem.

One refinement to "the only sane way": it's the only sane *operational/enforcement* model. There are two other positions, for different goals:

1. **Operational shadow (CHERI/FilC)** — provenance as location-keyed tags, cleared on non-pointer write. Sane when you must *run untrusted code safely* or *faithfully model* what provenance means dynamically.
2. **Static/logical (separation logic, RustBelt, your contracts)** — provenance/separation never appears in operational state *at all*; it's ghost resources / proved preconditions. Sane when you *author disciplined code and prove it correct* (no forgery to defend against, because you proved there is none).
3. **LLVM's abstract-spec provenance** — provenance as a nondeterministic logical token whose only job is to *justify optimizations* without committing to a runtime representation. This is the contentious one, precisely because it's neither an operational shadow nor fully static — it has to be sound w.r.t. *both* the optimizer and a flat machine, and that's the hard middle.

For your project this clarifies where each belongs. You're in position (2): IL code is authored under discipline and disjointness is a *proved* precondition, so you don't need operational provenance — there's nothing to dynamically catch. CHERI/FilC's shadow is the right tool when (a) the bottom target is *actually CHERI* (then the tagged memory is free and you'd model it directly — and you'd get spatial+temporal safety for the whole bootstrapped system as a bonus), or (b) libc code does genuine pointer↔int round-trips or pointer *comparison/hashing* (`qsort` with pointer keys, pointer-keyed maps, `memcmp` over pointer-containing structs) — *that's* where provenance bites, and the shadow model is the clean way to give those operations a semantics.

And it sharpens the earlier `Word → Option (state × Byte)` idea: if you ever do go to a shadow-memory model, the cell-tag should be a **capability/provenance tag with the clear-on-non-pointer-store rule (CHERI/FilC)** — *not* a borrow-state automaton. Provenance has a clean, local, operational shadow semantics; borrow/exclusivity (temporal) does not — it belongs in the logic (RustBelt/Iris ghost state). So the right split is:

- **provenance → parallel/shadow RAM** (operational, CHERI/FilC), if and when you need operational pointer integrity;
- **shared/unique borrows → logical ghost resources** (your contracts / fractional permissions), kept out of operational memory.

Two different concerns, two different homes — and your FilC observation is exactly the argument for why provenance is the one that *does* deserve to live in (parallel) memory.

### 👤 **User**  ·  02:37:50; waited 15m01s

if they do the same thing, can they be mapped to each other?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  02:38:54; model 1m23s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Yes — but "map" here means an *interpretation / soundness theorem*, not a literal identity. And there are actually two different mappings being asked about, because we've been conflating two axes: *operational-vs-logical* (where the discipline lives) and *provenance-vs-borrows* (what the discipline tracks). Both are mappable, each with a known shape and a known obstruction.

## Operational shadow ↔ logical ghost (same discipline, two representations)

These are mappable, and the map is exactly **Iris-style adequacy / RustBelt**. The logic (separation logic, fractional permissions, borrow/lifetime propositions) is the *abstract* side; the shadow memory (`Word → (tag, byte)`, CHERI/FilC tags) is one *concrete model*. The bridge is a **resource/state interpretation** plus a soundness theorem:

- You define the resource algebra so that *owning the logical resource* "unique borrow of region `r`" corresponds to *"the shadow tags on `r` say owned/unique"* — Iris calls this `state_interp σ`, tying ghost state to the physical/instrumented state σ.
- You prove the proof rules sound against the instrumented steps. RustBelt does precisely this: a semantic model of Rust's borrow/lifetime types in Iris over the λRust operational semantics.

One direction is always available — **logic → model (soundness):** the shadow memory is *a* model of the logic; logical facts can be read off the instrumented state. The reverse (completeness — every non-trapping execution yields a derivation) is harder and usually only partial. The practical consequence for you: you don't have to choose religiously. **Author and prove in the logical coordinate** (your `Wf`/`Disjoint` contracts — which are just the degenerate instantiation where the "shadow" is trivial and the resources are plain `Prop`s), and *if* you ever need the operational coordinate (an executable Miri/FilC-style checker, or a CHERI target), connect them with one adequacy theorem instead of rebuilding.

## Provenance (capabilities) ↔ borrows (shared/unique)

Mappable too, but **only modulo the substructural axis** — and that's the real content of "if they do the same thing."

- A capability ≈ the **region/provenance** part of a borrow (which object + bounds). That part corresponds cleanly: provenance ↔ `Slice`.
- But plain capabilities and borrows differ on **exclusivity = affine-vs-copyable**. An ordinary capability is a *freely copyable value* — you can hold many caps to the same object; caps alone enforce *no* exclusivity. A `unique` borrow is *affine* — you can't duplicate it; uniqueness *is* the non-duplicability. So a naive bijection fails exactly here.

Match the linearity and they line up:

| borrow | capability |
|---|---|
| `shared &` (duplicable, read) | ordinary (copyable) capability |
| `unique &mut` (affine, exclusive) | **linear capability** |

CHERI has explored exactly **linear capabilities** for this (exclusive ownership / safe reuse / revocation). So *capabilities + a permission-and-linearity lattice* ≈ the full borrow lattice; *plain provenance* ↔ only the region. Conversely, borrows *encode* provenance for free (a borrow's region is its provenance; unforgeability becomes "you can only obtain a borrow legitimately" — your type system / proof). So borrows are the **more expressive coordinate**: they subsume provenance's region and add exclusivity + lifetime.

## The unifying picture

They feel mappable because they're all instances of one thing — *which agent may access which memory, when* — differing on three coordinates:

1. **what's tracked:** region only (provenance) vs region + exclusivity + lifetime (borrows);
2. **where it lives:** operational/enforced (CHERI/FilC shadow) vs logical/proved (SL/Iris);
3. **value discipline:** copyable (caps) vs affine (unique borrows).

Pick coordinates on those axes and you can translate; the translation is an *interpretation + soundness theorem*, never a literal equality. The obstructions are precise: between operational and logical it's *completeness* (easy one way); between provenance and borrows it's *linearity* (match it and they correspond).

For the project this is the freeing conclusion: stay in the **logical, affine, region+exclusivity coordinate** (borrow contracts — cheapest, most expressive, machine-matching), and treat CHERI/FilC provenance and LLVM `noalias` as *lower-coordinate projections* your borrow contract can *generate soundly* — rather than as separate disciplines you'd have to reconcile. A unique borrow, proved, is exactly what makes a linear capability sound below it and a `noalias` attribute sound beside it.

### 👤 **User**  ·  04:54:02; waited 2h14m

how do return values handled in LowIR?

### 👤 **User**  ·  04:54:45; waited 42.5s

how do return values handled in LowIR? what's the expectation about register sharing/isolation across function calls?

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  04:55:37; model 1m12s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

Two honest answers about the current model, then what the *expectation* should be.

## Return values

There's **no first-class return-value mechanism.** Recall `.call g` runs the callee body on the caller's *shared* `St` and threads the callee's final state back (catching `.ret`→`.normal`):

```
| .call g => match exec fuel g s with | some (s', .ret) => some (s', .normal) | other => other
```

So a "return value" is just **whatever register (or memory) the callee leaves its result in, per its spec** — the caller reads it from the shared post-state. In the demo, `loadByteFn` leaves the byte in `x12` and the caller reads `x12`; nothing in the IL designates `x12` as "the return register." Which register carries the result is a *convention recorded in the callee's spec*, not enforced by `.call`. The ABI return register (`a0`/`x10` on RISC-V) only appears at `compile_sim`. Note also there's no `ra` (return address) at the IL — control return is the `ret`-catching semantics, not a jump-to-saved-address, so `ra` is purely a `compile_sim` concern.

## Register sharing/isolation across calls

Currently: **full sharing, zero isolation.** The callee executes on the caller's exact register file; it may read or write *any* register, and whatever it writes is visible to the caller afterward. There is **no callee-saved/caller-saved split, no save/restore, no frame** — i.e. no calling convention at the IL level. The caller learns what survives a call **only from the callee's spec** — its *footprint*: which registers it clobbers vs preserves. In the demo, the caller's `x11`/`x13` survive the call to `loadByteFn` simply because `loadByteFn`'s body (and hence spec) only touches `x10`/`x12`.

The key reason this is *fine* without a convention: the register file is **unbounded (`Reg = Nat`)**, so **isolation is achievable by register-set disjointness rather than save/restore.** A caller keeping a value in `x20` is automatically safe across a callee that only uses `x10`/`x12` — caller-live and callee-used register sets just don't overlap, and with ∞ registers they never *have* to. That's precisely why the IL needs no calling convention: there's no register pressure forcing reuse, so there's nothing to clobber and nothing to save.

## The expectation (what the contract should say)

Make it explicit at the spec level: a function's contract declares **(reads, clobbers, preserves)** over registers, plus its memory footprint/frame. Then:

- **Return values ⊆ clobbers**, designated by convention (the spec says "result in `x12`").
- **Callers must not assume preservation of the clobber set**; they may rely on the preserved-set. The demo's `loadByteFn_spec` is exactly this contract (writes `x12`, preserves the rest, memory untouched), and the caller composes against it.
- The IL **enforces nothing** about this — the discipline lives entirely in the specs (which is the CompCert/CakeML choice: keep high IRs convention-agnostic).

And where the convention finally bites: **`compile_sim`.** When register allocation squeezes the ∞ IL registers into 32 physical ones, reuse *does* force clobbering — *that's* when the ABI's callee-saved/caller-saved split + stack save/restore must **realize the IL spec's preserved-set promises** (allocate a preserved IL reg to a callee-saved physical reg, or have the caller spill it). The IL says *what* survives; the ABI lowering makes it *true*. Return value → the ABI return register there.

## One optional extension

If you want footprints partly enforced *at the IL* rather than only in specs, give `.call` explicit args/results — LLVM-style `call g (args…) → (results…)` — so the call node itself names what's passed and returned, and the "everything else preserved" is structural. That's a future refinement; the current shared-state `.call` + spec-stated footprint is the minimal version, and the demo shows it's enough for modular, disjointness-aware reasoning.

**In one line:** return values and register isolation are both *spec/convention* matters at the IL (shared register file, footprints in specs, isolation via register-set disjointness enabled by ∞ registers); the actual ABI convention — callee-saved/caller-saved, save/restore, return register, `ra` — is introduced and proved at `compile_sim`, with the ABI as a parameter.

### 👤 **User**  ·  04:58:59; waited 3m03s

zero isolation seems very hard for reasoning. you have to reason across two function state.

### 🤖 **Assistant**  ·  05:00:04; model 1m23s

> 💭 (thinking)
> 

You're right, and it's the correct critique — but the fix is *frame-based specs*, not operational isolation. Let me separate the two, because the demo conflated them.

**Operational isolation isn't real and isn't the answer.** At runtime there's one register file and one memory; a callee "not seeing" the caller's registers is a fiction. CompCert/CakeML don't isolate either — they reason about the *shared* store. So the goal isn't to hide state; it's to reason modularly *over* shared state. That's the entire reason separation logic exists.

**The demo was non-modular because its spec was a full-state transformer.** `loadByteFn_spec` says `exec … s = some (s.rset 12 v, .normal)` — the *entire* post-state, exactly. That only reads as "modular" because the callee is one instruction, so the full transformer is tiny and the caller can eyeball that `x11`/`x13`/memory are untouched. For any real callee, threading the complete `s'` and inspecting it is precisely the "reason across two function states" problem you're naming.

**The fix: footprint + frame.** State the callee's spec only over what it touches, and *declare* what it leaves alone:

```
spec g :  ∃ st', exec _ g s = some (st', _)
        ∧ <effect on the footprint>                    -- e.g. st'.rget 12 = …
        ∧ (∀ r, r ∉ W → st'.rget r = s.rget r)         -- register frame (W = clobber set)
        ∧ (∀ a, ¬ S.has a → st'.mem a = s.mem a)        -- memory frame (S = write slice)
```

The last two conjuncts **are** the frame. Now a caller that holds a value in `x20` and owns a buffer `B` learns they survive the call by *instantiating the ∀'s* — `x20 ∉ W`, `B ⊥ S` — and gets `st'.rget 20 = s.rget 20`, `st'.mem` on `B` unchanged. It never inspects the callee's full state; it reasons only about the small footprint plus its own disjoint frame. That's logical isolation over a shared store — modularity without hiding anything.

**This machinery is already in your codebase — the demo just didn't expose it at the call interface.** Two things make it land cleanly:

- The disjointness the register/memory frame needs (`r ∉ W`, `B ⊥ S`) is exactly the **borrow `Slice`/`Disjoint`** contract. So the frame rule and the separation discipline are the same investment.
- `hex0`'s proof is *full* of frame-style preservation clauses already: every `body_*` lemma carries `Regs st'` preservation (registers outside the scratch set unchanged) and a memory frame ("input region unchanged"). `main_loop` literally maintains "memory outside the output slice is preserved." Those are frame conditions in disguise. The fix is to make `.call`'s *spec interface* use that same form instead of a full-state transformer.

**For registers specifically**, the frame is the preserved-set (complement of the clobber set `W`), and the ∞-register point means a caller's live registers are in the frame *by construction* (it allocated them outside the callee's `W`) — so the register frame instantiation is usually trivial. **For memory**, the frame is "outside the write slices," discharged by `Disjoint` via the borrow contract.

So: keep the shared-state operational `.call` (don't add isolation), but **(1) write function specs in footprint+frame form, and (2) prove a frame lemma once** — "anything disjoint from a function's declared footprint is preserved across a call to it." Then callers compose by framing, and the "reason across two states" problem disappears. The earlier message's full-state `loadByteFn_spec` was the shortcut; the frame form is what scales.

Want me to refactor the demo to the frame-style spec + a generic frame lemma, so the modular composition is explicit (and stress-test that it really avoids cross-function-state reasoning)? That's the concrete thing that would either confirm this is ergonomic or surface where it needs more structure — which is exactly the "is it doable or needs more factoring" test, applied to calls.
